Master packet · GFiber v8 transformed to Amazon Leadership Principles · STAR format · math, solution design and probe questions intact

Amazon — Supply Chain Manager III (L6) — the MASTER packet

You. Rajesh Rishiyur Chandrasekaran
What this document is. The GFiber MASTER packet v8 (Sep 13, 2026 — 255 pages) transformed for the Amazon L6 SCM loop: the same eighteen stories in the same uniform shape — 60-second pitch → SITUATION / TASK (the full problem space: what and why, when, scope, scale, and the metrics to improve with their baselines) → ACTION (the solution space: five gated phases, each with entry · delivered · exit bar, plus how it was done and who was influenced, in prose) → RESULTS (the Situation metrics, improved: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality, tied back to the stated baselines) → one PROBING section per story (challenge Q&A, the phase deep-probe bank, the business-impact model with KPI tree and numbers table, the fully worked derivation tables — the problem in numbers, the fix with every value substituted, the metric before → after, and the dollars with every multiplication shown — the solution flow (the system & data map), sensitivity (low/base/high), the spoken total-impact statement and the full "if challenged" answers) — mapped to Amazon's sixteen Leadership Principles instead of a JD. §3 converts the twelve system-design prompts into design engagements told from the Elementum years, each keeping its stocks-and-flows map, first-principles invariants, solution design, system map, math and deep-dive probes. §4 is the numbers ledger — one canonical value per metric — and the never-say list. This document is self-contained: nothing requires going back to the source packet.
Relation to the loop packet. This is the depth book; amazon-l6-scm-loop-packet.pdf is the loop-mechanics book (interviewer tracker, AIQB 128-question bank, writing-exercise defense, video execution). The crosswalk — including the places where the two documents currently disagree — is in §1.3. Resolve those conflicts before the loop; never carry both versions of a number into the same building.
Voice, held everywhere. TPM / SAP integration program lead. "I" throughout. SAP teams and configurators built to the signed spec; I owned the data contract, the definitions, the gates, the signatures and the calls. Never claim to configure or deploy SAP. The only platform personally configured: Manhattan DOM. The MIT Supply Chain MicroMasters + Supply Chain Network Design certificate is an acquired toolkit, labeled wherever it appears.
Basis labels, used on every number. record — in the program record, say it flat · user anchor — a figure a named stakeholder stated, attributed · industry benchmark — a published rate, named · modeled — computed from stated inputs; say "modeled" in the same breath, never promote it to record.

0. How to use this packet in the loop

  1. Reading order = prep order: §2 is arranged as employer blocks in prep priority — Sephora (current role) first, then Flexport, then Elementum (with the Walmart-client engagements S19–S21 and the twelve design engagements), Manhattan last. Prep front-to-back: the front blocks carry the 3-hour loop day (Thursday), the back blocks round out the 2-hour day (Friday). Story codes are unchanged, so every cross-reference still points where it always did.
  2. Night before: §1 LP map and roster → §4 never-say list → skim each story's pitch line.
  3. In the room, unprompted: the 60-SECOND PITCH only. It ends on "Primary metric — X, before → after, $ impact." Stop there. The interviewer's probes are how they fill their STAR worksheet; silence after the pitch works for you.
  4. "Walk me through it": STAR. The Action is five gated phases — Discovery → Plan & Design → Build & Test → Cutover/Go-Live → Hypercare — each spoken as entry bar · what I delivered · exit bar.
  5. "How did you actually run it": the PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A in each story's PROBING section. Answer the phase asked, then stop.
  6. "How would you design…": §3. Open with the stocks-and-flows map, state the invariants, then the design, then the metric. Program methodology lives only in §3's shared section — never inside an employer story.
  7. Numbers: §4. A record dollar is said flat. Where the record holds no dollar, say the operational metric and then the modeled figure with the word "modeled" in the same breath — each story's Business impact model carries the inputs, the math and the low / base / high range. Never promote a modeled number to record.
  8. Amazon loop mechanics: five 55-minute video interviews, each interviewer assigned 2–3 Leadership Principles, STAR worksheet, notes compared afterward. One question, one story — never the same story twice across the loop. The LP map below is the routing table.

Tell me about yourself · Why Amazon — the two scripted openers

Adapted from the loop packet's §2 into this packet's voice and numbers. Where the two documents disagree (§1.3), every figure below is this packet's §4 value — the loop packet's copy of these scripts should be corrected to match, never the reverse. The beats map to this packet's codes: Sephora → S1/S18, Flexport → S11, Elementum → S8/S6, Manhattan → S13 — so any follow-up probe has a full story behind it. Say the long TMAY once, early in the loop; interviewers two through five get the 30-second version.

Tell me about yourself — newest to oldest, about 75 seconds

"I've spent fifteen years on one problem — making sure inventory is where the demand is and that the promise made on it holds. Four companies, most recent first.

Sephora, today. We moved the inventory book of record onto S/4HANA, and Store, Retail DC and Dotcom each meant something different by 'available' — most oversell bugs are vocabulary bugs. I owned the definitions and got one signed availability number per node per channel, reconciled daily against the book; customer-facing oversell incidents went from recurring to zero through two go-lives. I also ran the dual-DC WMS migration that unblocked a $20M project pipeline — eight months, on time, against a 0.5% reconciliation tolerance published before the first mock.

Flexport, before that. Leadership asked for a weekly review of shipment tracking and nobody owned it. I built it, ran it with the VP every week for a year, then automated the data under it. Cost to serve went from sixty-six cents a container to three — in Finance's loaded rates, not mine — while the base grew from seven customers to sixty-eight behind published graduation gates.

Elementum. Supply-chain software for manufacturers. I replaced carrier-brochure lead times with measured lane distributions on two hundred shipping lanes — planning to the slow day, not the average — which held 99% lead-time accuracy and released about a million and a half dollars of safety stock. I also drove forecast accuracy on the scoped SKUs from 70 to 85 percent — the first forecast the planners trusted enough to act on in their weekly planning meeting.

Manhattan Associates, where I started. Allocation and order promising for a retailer with three mega-DCs and a hundred stores. Through Cyber Monday we held 99% fill against a 95% goal — roughly eight million dollars of orders protected, and that figure is the program record.

I haven't run an Amazon network region, and I won't pretend otherwise. What I have done four times is design the supply chain — the lead times, the buffers, the one definition of inventory — and keep it honest in a weekly review. I want to do that at network scale."

30-second version (interviewers two through five may ask again): Sephora — one signed definition of "available" across every node on S/4HANA; oversells to zero. Flexport — built and ran the weekly review; sixty-six cents to three a container. Elementum — measured lanes, promise at the tail, about $1.5M of safety stock out at 99% lead-time accuracy. Manhattan — 99% fill through peak. Then the last two sentences of the long version, unchanged.

Why Amazon — about 60 seconds, usually only the hiring manager asks

"Three reasons.

First, the forecast. At Elementum, two hundred shipping lanes were planned on the carrier's promised transit time. I replaced that with what actually happened on each lane — the distribution, not the brochure — and promised at the 99th percentile. That released about a million and a half dollars of inventory, and the shortage alert built on that data was the first one planners actually acted on. Amazon plans the whole network off a forecast; I've done the work of making people trust one.

Second, the weekly review. At Flexport, leadership asked for a weekly review of shipment tracking and nobody owned it. I built it and ran it with the VP every week for a year; cost to serve went from sixty-six cents a container to three, in Finance's rates. That input-metric, output-metric weekly review is how Amazon runs. I've already lived that way.

Third, one version of the truth. At Sephora I own how inventory is counted and promised across stores, the warehouses and dotcom. Three node types meant three different things by 'available'; I got it to one signed number per node per channel, checked daily, and customer-facing oversells went to zero. Amazon's promise to the customer rests on that same one number, at a thousand times the scale.

Why now: what I went to Sephora to build is live — the migration, the omni DC, the promising governance. I'm not running from anything; this is the biggest version of the same problem."

PROBING — the short follow-ups these two scripts draw.

What's inside "sixty-six cents to three"?

Cost to serve per tracked container, computed in Finance's loaded rates — their model, not mine. Behind it: tracking automation 40% → 90%, system accuracy 60% → 90%, and the customer base 7 → 68 (9.7×) while the review stayed weekly. The full walk is S11; the ~$1.5M of pre-production measurement savings is a separate number and never merged with it.

How much of that review did you run by hand?

At the start, about 1.5 to 2 hours a week of my own hands across four systems — and heading toward roughly 4 as volume grew, which is exactly why I wrote the BRD and automated it (S16). The value of the automation was scale without headcount, not my hours back.

You said $1.5M at Elementum — your roster also shows a $2M. Same money?

No, and I never add them. The ~$1.5M (580K units) is the safety stock released by the 200-lane measured-lead-time program (S8). The $2M is the separate release from the real-time supply/demand view as forecast accuracy went 70 → 85% (S6). Both are record; they stay apart.

Whose number is the eight million?

The program record from the Cyber Monday event — roughly $6M at risk when backorders piled up, about $8M of orders protected by close at 99% fill against the 95% goal. The decomposition into margin and cancel cost is my model, and I say so when I use it (S13).

Why are you leaving Sephora?

I've delivered what I went there to do — the WMS migration, the omni DC and the promising governance are live. I'm running toward scale, not away from anything: at Sephora a point of promise accuracy is thousands of orders; here it's millions.

Which of the four chapters would you pick for a deep dive?

Sephora, because it's current and I own it end to end — S1 is that walk. If the interviewer owns operating cadence, the Flexport review (S11); if planning or network design, the Elementum lanes (S8).

2. The stories — employer blocks in prep-priority order: pitch, gated STAR, phase probes, and the impact math

Every story now follows the Amazon worksheet shape: 60-second pitch (say first, unprompted; ends on the primary metric) → SITUATION / TASK as the full problem space — what and why, when, the initial scope, the scale, the challenges, the risks if nothing happened, and the metrics to improve with their baseline values → ACTION as the solution space — five gated phases (entry · delivered · exit) plus how it was done and who was influenced, in prose → RESULTS — the Situation metrics, improved, quantified as $ · # · % · time · customer · quality and tied back to the stated baselines → one PROBING section per story: the challenge and results Q&A, the PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK (answer the phase asked, then stop), the business-impact model (KPI tree, numbers table), the fully worked derivation tables — the problem in numbers, the fix with every value substituted, the metric before → after, the dollars with every multiplication shown — the solution flow, the sensitivity table, the spoken total-impact statement and the full "if challenged" answers. The GFiber fit line is replaced by the Amazon LP mapping and a "where this lands at Amazon" sentence. Dates and counts inside each story are the program record; anything labeled illustrative exists only to make the math complete.

Order. The stories are grouped by employer in prep priority — Sephora (current role) first, then Flexport, then Elementum (closing with the Walmart-client engagements S19–S21 and the twelve §3 design engagements), Manhattan Associates last. Every story code and every word inside each story is unchanged from the previous edition — only the order moved, so the §1.3 crosswalk and every cross-reference (e.g., "the S18 discipline") still point where they always did.

Employer block 1 — Sephora (current role)S1 S/4HANA ↔ ISM one signed "available" · S2 UDC comingle + robotics gate · S18 RP → JDA WMS migration · S3 SNB de-aggregation · S4 the in-transit rule (failure story) · S5 the architecture council · S15 the 4-week cadence

Prep this block first — it is the current role and the program family interviewers probe hardest for recency. S1, S2 and S18 open the block because they carry the loop's most likely questions (one definition of available, the omni-DC launch, the migration discipline); S18 sits with its Sephora program family even though the roster once read it last.

S1 — Sephora S/4HANA ↔ ISM: one signed meaning of "available" across Store, Retail DC and Dotcom DC — policy before configuration

Amazon LPs: Earn Trust (backup) · Ownership (backup) · Think Big (third — the book-of-record migration) · Broad Responsibility (oversell is a customer harm). Crosswalk: loop packet S2 — same program, consistent. Recency: current role.

Honesty label. Integration program lead — no system reported to me; every configurator sat in another org. I owned the definitions, the asymmetry, the decrement and reconciliation contract, the signature chain and the council ask; engineers configured and coded to the FSD; planners executed the business acts.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Sephora was moving the inventory book of record from the in-house MCS to SAP S/4HANA, with ISM stood up beside it for the real-time view; Store, Retail DC and Dotcom DC each meant something different by 'available,' and most oversell bugs are vocabulary bugs. Task. As the integration program lead for inventory supply — no system reported to me — I had to define every bucket, its owning system, and how available-for-sale is computed and decremented in real time, and get Planning, Finance and operations to sign it before configuration. Action. Discovery: a bucket inventory with one proposed owner per bucket; the bar was every bucket owned. Plan & Design: the lower-of-two basis, the AFS equation, the real-time decrement contract with idempotent events, daily reconciliation to S/4 — FSD v1.1 → v1.2 → signed 6/5/25 by three orgs. Build & Test: node × channel × bucket-transition matrix; E2E Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30. Cutover: January planning go-live, then May execution; OMS reads published AFS, never computes; MCS retired as book of record. Hypercare: daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift; control owners on Negative AFS. Lesson: policy before configuration. Result. Primary metric: customer-facing oversell incidents — recurring across three node types → zero through two go-lives; one signed AFS per node per channel. $ impact: ≈$2.5M/yr — $1M measured MCS support cost retired, plus ≈$1.5M modeled (avoided oversell, dual-book reconciliation labor, legacy change premium); the record has no pre-state oversell dollar, so the $1.5M is my model and I say so."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Sephora moved the book of record from MCS to S/4HANA, with ISM beside it to see digital demand S/4 cannot see in real time — a web order placed, a BOPIS order packed, a POS sale rung but not yet posted. Three node types, three working definitions of "available." Merchandising wanted merchandise reserve visible, Finance wanted on-hand to tie to the book, dotcom wanted in-transit promisable. Nobody owned the definition; I owned no system.

T — Own the integration design and data contract of inventory supply: which buckets exist, which system owns each, how available-for-sale is computed per node, and how it feeds promising, replenishment and Finance.

Most oversell bugs are vocabulary bugs, and that is why this mattered: the goal, written with the VP Supply Chain in the sponsor memo, was one signed AFS per node per channel, one Financial On-Hand, and zero customer-impacting incidents. Left unfixed, the risks were recurring vocabulary-driven oversells — a customer harm, not a ledger error — two books reconciled by hand, the MCS run cost continuing, and a legacy change premium on an undocumented stack; vocabulary drift — a bucket without an owner — sat at the top of my risk register.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: customer-facing oversell incidents — recurring across three node types; target zero. Availability truths — two books reconciled by hand; target one signed AFS per node per channel, with daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift as the standing check. MCS — still running as book of record; target retired.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (record dates: kickoff 3/29 → council 5/15 → LOE 7/2 → sign-off 7/31 → FSD 8/14 → peak freeze Oct–Dec; ISM FSD v1.1 3/5/25 → v1.2 5/16/25 → signed 6/5/25; E2E Inbound 11/22 · Dotcom 12/20 · Retail 1/30; issues log 836 rows / 85.4% closed. The dated gate chain, the issues log and the rollback triggers are program-level artifacts shared with S2/S5 — one umbrella program; if two of these stories go to one loop, say so):

  1. Discovery — profiled both books (MCS and S/4) for SKU overlap and order profiles per node; node-by-node sessions with ~15 functions; built the bucket inventory with one proposed owning system per bucket. Exit bar: every bucket owned by exactly one system; business sessions closed 4/30; open questions carried with owner and date.
  2. Plan & Design — wrote the lower-of-two basis, the AFS/FOH equations, the real-time decrement contract (event → bucket delta, idempotency key per event), the daily ISM-vs-MB52 reconciliation into FSD v1.1 → v1.2, the RAID log and the council pack (S5). Exit bar: FSD signed 6/5/25 by Supply Chain Strategy, Inventory Management and FSC Inventory Accounting with zero open "who owns this bucket" rows.
  3. Build & Test — authored the node × channel × bucket-transition matrix with before/after assertions; SIT on the interface contracts before UAT; published the E2E chain dates. Exit bar: 0 open contract defects in SIT; E2E green 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30; Blocker + Critical open = 0 at go/no-go.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — wrote the go/no-go recommendation from counted rows, the publish-switch plan (OMS reads ISM AFS, never computes) and the reserve-copy invariants; assigned control-screen roles. Exit bar: January go-live (reserve rules live against demand), May go-live (execution); no SKU with two published AFS values; Negative AFS clean at T+1.
  5. Hypercare / Close — daily drift report (ISM vs MB52), Negative AFS / AFS Correction audit review, hypercare exit memo and FSD as-built note. Exit bar: drift = 0 SKUs on consecutive days; blockers/day under threshold; exited into the monthly release train.

Every configurator sat in another org: SAP teams and configurators built to the signed spec, planners confirmed in UAT, and control owners ran the Negative AFS and AFS Correction screens after handoff. Mine: the bucket inventory, the definitions and the asymmetry, the decrement and reconciliation contract, the node × channel × bucket-transition matrix design, the go/no-go recommendation and the signature chain. The unique value was taking the definition nobody owned and getting it signed before configuration — the lower-of-two basis, the AFS equation and the real-time decrement contract written into an FSD three orgs signed on 6/5/25.

The obstacles were three orgs each defending a different truth about “available,” and sixty people to move without authority — overcome by pre-wiring every row of the systems-impacted table with its owner's level-of-effort before the council, running working sessions to one table instead of arbitrating in meetings, and converting objections into FSD rows. Priorities were set by slotting epics by business criticality × effort, with no story entering QA without a matrix cell reference; buy-in came from the pre-wiring — nobody learned their cost in the council room, and the council was asked for three decisions rather than a review. The decision I challenged: Merchandising wanted merch reserve promisable — I priced it (unpickable stock sold) and the no went into the FSD as a row, not a meeting minute; and I held the asymmetry itself — oversell is a customer defect, undersell a planning variance Inventory Control chases through the Negative AFS and AFS Correction screens.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: recurring vocabulary-driven oversells → zero through two go-lives; one signed AFS per node per channel; MCS retired. $ impact: ≈$2.5M/yr ($1M user anchor + ≈$1.5M modeled — model below).

  • $ — ≈$2.5M/yr — $1M MCS run/support cost retired (user anchor) + ≈$1.5M modeled (avoided oversell, dual-book reconciliation labor, legacy change premium); the record has no pre-state oversell dollar, so the $1.5M is my model and I say so.
  • # — Oversell incidents recurring across three node types → zero through two go-lives (record); dual-book reconciliation ≈4 FTE by hand → ≈1 FTE reading the drift report (modeled).
  • % — Issues log 85.4% of 836 rows closed (program-level artifact shared with S2/S5); the oversell rate behind the ≈$0.9M row is 0.10% of orders — modeled, benchmark 0.05–0.25%; the record movement is incidents → zero.
  • Time — Dated gate chain held: FSD v1.1 3/5/25 → v1.2 5/16/25 → signed 6/5/25; E2E Inbound 11/22 · Dotcom 12/20 · Retail 1/30; January planning and May execution go-lives (record dates).
  • Customer — Customer-facing oversell incidents zero through two go-lives; the asymmetry treats oversell as a customer defect and undersell as a planning variance (record).
  • Quality — One signed AFS per node per channel; no SKU with two published AFS values; daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift = 0 SKUs on consecutive days at hypercare exit (record).

Against the goal stated above: oversell incidents recurring → zero through two go-lives; two availability truths → one signed AFS per node per channel; MCS retired as book of record. The trade behind it was priced: the asymmetry accepts visible undersell to make oversell zero by construction, and merch reserve got visibility without promisability — unpickable stock sold was never worth the sale.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Earn Trust?

Because the deliverable was one signed truth across three orgs that did not report to me — Supply Chain Strategy, Inventory Management and FSC Inventory Accounting each signed the FSD that carried the definitions. One signature would have left two orgs free to disagree later.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Earn Trust?

S14 — the $2.5M OMS↔WMS discrepancy, transparency over a quiet fix — is my primary Earn Trust story; S3 (Finance and Ops on one number) and S5 (the council) carry the same LP. This story is the Earn Trust backup in my map.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is from my current role at Sephora — the FSD was signed 6/5/25 and the two go-lives ran January and May — so it is the most recent record I have for this principle.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Oversell was the customer-facing defect the whole program existed to kill, so incidents-to-zero and one signed AFS per node per channel lead. The other results that mattered: MCS retired as book of record, reconciliation moved from argument to a daily drift report with a named owner, and the E2E chain landing on its dates.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

There is no year-over-year revenue record here — the ≈$2.5M/yr is $1M of measured MCS run cost retired (user anchor) plus ≈$1.5M modeled against the pre-state baseline, not a YoY revenue change. The record movement is incidents: recurring → zero through two go-lives.

Probing — I'm concerned about the 0.10% oversell rate — tell me more.

I don't know it to the decimal — the record says incidents were recurring and went to zero; the rate is a benchmark set deliberately low, a twentieth of a typical e-commerce inventory-cancel rate. First thing I'd measure: the “item unavailable after order” cancel-reason code for the twelve months before January.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Policy before configuration is the lesson that held. Differently: the in-transit rule (S4) — back-test a ruleset against history before publishing it.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Every fulfillment promise rests on one published availability number per node that downstream systems read and never recompute — at Amazon scale that is the ATP/promise seam between IPC-style planning and the order pipeline: one definition, one owner, policy signed before code.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The signed criteria were one AFS per node per channel, one Financial On-Hand and zero customer-impacting incidents — all three held through both go-lives, with drift at zero SKUs on consecutive days at hypercare exit and MCS retired as book of record.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (15). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When every bucket on the table had exactly one proposed owning system and no open "who owns this" row. Sessions closed 4/30; the exit memo listed remaining questions with an owner and a date, not a hope.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote them with the VP Supply Chain in the sponsor memo — one signed AFS per node per channel, one Financial On-Hand, zero customer-impacting incidents. Supply Chain Strategy, Inventory Management and FSC Inventory Accounting signed the FSD that carried them.

What did you profile before designing?

Both books — MCS and S/4 — for SKU overlap and order profiles per node type, so the design argument started from their own data, not opinions about it.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Three at the top: vocabulary drift (a bucket without an owner), feed latency from WMS/OMS/POS, and override abuse on AFS-changing screens. Each had a trigger, a mitigation and an owner — mine was drift.

How did you handle an objection at the design review?

Merchandising wanted merch reserve promisable. I priced it — unpickable stock sold — and offered visibility without promisability. The no went into the FSD as a row, not into a meeting minute.

Why three signatories, not one?

Each owned a different truth: Strategy owned policy, Inventory Management owned execution, Inventory Accounting owned the book. One signature would have left two orgs free to disagree later.

Build & Test — How did you sequence stories in Jira?

Epics per module — reserve module, AFS engine, SNB, control screens — slotted by business criticality × effort; a story could not enter QA without a matrix cell reference.

How were UAT entrance criteria set and who could fail a build?

Entrance: SIT contract defects closed, test data handed off, environments ready. QA owned the gate and could fail a build; planners confirmed in UAT because QA had already demoed each matrix family during build.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The dated E2E days — Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30. Business and engineering ran the full flow in one room; every finding got a severity and owner before we broke.

Cutover — What made you willing to say go?

Counted rows: Blocker + Critical at zero, E2E green, the monitor for the interim copy built and tested, rollback triggers armed. Never confidence in the room.

What were your rollback triggers and did any fire?

Sync below 99% for four consecutive hours, or order-flow p95 above twice budget for an hour — consumers revert to the prior publish. Armed for both go-lives; never fired.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over?

Daily ISM-vs-MB52 drift and blockers/day under threshold on consecutive days, and the control owners — Inventory Control, Internal Audit, Finance — running the Negative AFS report without me. Then the design entered the monthly release train.

What did hypercare hand over, and to whom?

The control-owner map: Negative AFS to Inventory Control, Internal Audit and Finance; AFS Correction to the ISM app team; the daily drift report to Inventory Control. Every AFS-changing act had a screen, a role and an audit row before I stepped back.

Cross-phase — You owned no system; how did you get sixty people to sign?

Pre-wired every row of the systems-impacted table with its owner's level-of-effort before the council, ran working sessions to one table instead of arbitrating in meetings, and asked the council for three decisions rather than a review. A no that lives in a document survives; a no that lives in a meeting gets re-litigated weekly.

Why lower-of-two and not an average? · What would you do differently?

Oversell is a customer defect; undersell is a planning variance Inventory Control chases through the Negative AFS and AFS Correction screens — the asymmetry is the policy. Differently: the in-transit rule (S4) — back-test a ruleset against history before publishing it.

Where this lands at Amazon: every fulfillment promise rests on one published availability number per node that downstream systems read and never recompute — the exact contract I held the OMS to. At Amazon scale the same discipline is the ATP/promise seam between IPC-style planning and the order pipeline: one definition, one owner, policy signed before code.

Business impact model — KPI tree, numbers, the math

Gross margin + customer trust (primary outcome)
+- Oversell / cancel cost (flow, $/yr) -> 0 incidents
|    oversell = orders x vocab-driven oversell rate x cost/incident
|    (loop) oversell -> NPS down -> repeat rate down -> orders down
+- Dual-book reconciliation labor (stock: FTE) -> 1 FTE + drift report
|    (loop) more drift -> more manual recon -> slower fixes -> more drift
+- Legacy run cost (MCS support, $/yr) -> retired
+- Tech-debt change premium (changes/yr x extra hrs x rate) -> S/4 + ISM
+- Book accuracy -> unexplained shrink / audit-finding risk
+- Time-to-market for a new node or channel (weeks) -> a matrix row

"Three FTE sounds like a guess." — "It's a benchmark count. What's in the record is the mechanism: reconciliation moved from argument to a daily drift report with tolerance zero and a named owner. I'd size it with a two-week time study — how I sized the Austria effort at Elementum."

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Conservatively, about two and a half million dollars a year. One million of that is measured: the MCS support cost that went away when S/4 became the book of record. About nine hundred thousand is avoided oversell: on roughly thirty million dotcom orders, a tenth of a percent going wrong at about thirty dollars each, and the record shows those incidents went to zero through both go-lives. About three hundred thousand is reconciliation labor, three analysts who stopped reconciling two books by hand, and about three hundred thousand is the change premium we stopped paying on an undocumented stack. The first number is measured; the other three are my model, and I can show you every input."

S2 — Sephora UDC: two warehouses in one building → one comingle pool, coupled with a BlueYonder upgrade and robotics behind a published gate

Amazon LPs: Insist on the Highest Standards (primary — the 98% gate) · Deliver Results · Have Backbone (the throttle) · Earth's Best Employer (the floor protected). Crosswalk: loop packet S11 + S24 — consistent. Recency: current role.

Honesty label. I owned discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging and the gates; BlueYonder, Locus and the platform teams built and configured; DC Ops ran the floor. The +15% is Sephora's signed number; every dollar is my model on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Sephora's Utah DC ran as two systemic warehouses in one building, with a BlueYonder upgrade and Locus robotics on the same path and zero customer-facing incidents allowed. Task. I owned discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging, and the gates — a dozen core systems, 20+ touched, 60+ stakeholders. Action. Discovery: both books profiled; systems-impacted table with owners. Plan & Design: one comingle pool, three planning identities, two go-lives so each carried one defect class. Build & Test: node × channel × bucket matrix; robotics gate published at 98% throughput with a throttle built in advance. Cutover: six-step May choreography — clear negatives, transfer AFS to Uncommitted, scan-and-reserve, zero-then-publish so nothing was sellable in two places. Hypercare: week two hit 94; I throttled bots to ~30% of volume, root-caused zone/slotting logic, full volume week three. Lesson: a gate that cannot stop the program is decoration — mine did. Result. Primary metric: DC processing efficiency — baseline → +15% after one comingle pool replaced two systemic warehouses; customer-facing incidents zero through both go-lives. $ impact: ≈$1.6M/yr modeled (labor at +15%, pooling carry, fewer split shipments, self-transfer handling removed) — the +15% is the signed number; the dollars are my model."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The Utah DC ran as two systemic warehouses — Dotcom 801 (selling location 800) and Retail 901 — transferring stock to itself and paying duplicate handling for zero customer value. The ask: one omni/comingle facility, with a BlueYonder WMS 8.2 → 2023.1 upgrade and Locus robotics on the same critical path, a dozen core systems (20+ touched), an Oct–Dec peak freeze, zero customer-facing incidents allowed.

T — Discovery through FSD for the location and inventory model, the staging, and the acceptance gates; 60+ stakeholders on the steering path.

The challenges sat on the critical path itself: the WMS upgrade and the robotics ramp shared it, the Oct–Dec peak freeze bounded it, and the interim would run two live truths. Doing nothing meant continuing to pay duplicate handling on self-transfers; running the program carried the register's top three risks — two live truths during the interim, a robotics ramp with no stop condition, and a peak collision — each given a trigger, a mitigation and an owner before sign-off.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: processing efficiency — index 100 (units per labor-hour), the pre-UDC baseline DC Ops would measure against post-stabilization; customer-facing incidents — zero allowed, the constraint every other number had to move inside; the robotics ramp — a 98% gate published in advance, set from the pre-ramp baseline and the incident budget before the event; self-transfers and duplicate handling — the standing cost one pool exists to remove.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (record: kickoff 3/29 → sessions 4/30 → council 5/15 → LOE 7/2 → sign-off 7/31 → FSD 8/14 → peak freeze → E2E 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30 → Locus RTL Feb–Mar, slipped 3 weeks, re-slotted openly → January go-live → May go-live; cutover plan 343 tasks / 48,885 task-minutes ≈ 815 hrs; issues log 836 rows / 85.4% closed, Blocker 164 + Critical 277 = 52.8%):

  1. Discovery (3/29 → 4/30) — profiled 801/901 (SKU overlap, order profiles); sponsor objectives and success criteria (zero customer-facing incidents; one comingle facility); systems-impacted table. Exit bar: every impacted system named to an owner; success criteria signed by steering; open questions dated.
  2. Plan & Design (5/15 → 8/14) — council pack (three decisions + out-of-scope fence, S5); the 900/901 location/inventory model with three planning identities; staged two-go-live timeline; RAID log; test-matrix skeleton; FSD to 8/14. Exit bar: council sign-off 7/31, FSD 8/14, peak-freeze boundary decided, LOE-backed plan across ~30 build tracks.
  3. Build & Test (Aug → Jan/Apr) — node × channel × bucket-transition matrix as the regression asset; SIT contract-defect list; dated E2E chain; UAT entrance memo; Locus gate definition (98%) with throttle spec; three mock cutovers against the 0.5% tolerance — the S18 discipline, reused across the same Sephora program family. Exit bar: E2E green three dates; Blocker + Critical = 0 at each go/no-go; mock 3 inside tolerance; Locus RTL retested after the slip.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (Jan; May 1 → Jul 1) — cutover runbook (343 tasks, owner + duration + predecessor per task); six-step May choreography; go/no-go recommendation; rollback plan per interface. Exit bar: six invariants held (Σ AFS conserved; no SKU sellable on 800 and 900 at once; negatives cleared first); rollback never fired.
  5. Hypercare / Close (Jun → Jul) — daily burn-down read (271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4), Locus weekly gate read, hypercare exit memo, template handed to the next DC. Exit bar: blockers/day < 2 sustained → exit week 3 of 4; Locus at full volume week 3; +15% measured post-stabilization by DC Ops.

BlueYonder, Locus and the platform teams built and configured; DC Ops ran the floor and measured the +15% post-stabilization. Mine specifically: the location/inventory model, the staging, the gates, the council pack, the cutover runbook (343 tasks with owner, duration and predecessor) and the go/no-go recommendation. The contribution that made one pool workable: three planning identities over one physical location — one physical pool serving both channels without blurring anyone's lens — plus the published 98% robotics gate with the throttle dial built in advance, and the staged two-go-live timeline so each go-live carried one defect class.

The obstacles: the Locus RTL slipped three weeks — escalated the week it surfaced, re-slotted openly, and I refused to compress UAT to absorb it; Digital worried a virtual Retail DC would blur their forecasting lens — the three-identity design made the concern the design, not an argument. Priorities ran as ~30 build tracks under one log, stories slotted by business criticality × effort, Blocker + Critical open = 0 as the gate; buy-in came from steering (DC Ops, IT, Supply Chain Strategy) signing the success criteria in the council pack, with DC Ops owning the robotics gate and the dial before the ramp — the rule fired, I didn't improvise. I challenged the single-go-live instinct — two go-lives so we never hunted two defect classes in the same week — and held the comingle-reserve improvements out of the release to protect zero incidents, priced as a later enhancement at steering.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: processing efficiency +15% (record, Sephora signed); zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives; robotics gate held at 94 vs 98, full volume week three. $ impact: ≈$1.6M/yr modeled + ≈$2.2M one-time safety-stock release (model below).

  • $ — ≈$1.6M/yr modeled (labor at +15%, pooling carry, fewer split shipments, self-transfer handling removed) + ≈$2.2M one-time safety-stock release modeled via √2 pooling; the +15% is the signed number, the dollars are my model.
  • # — Cutover runbook 343 tasks / 48,885 task-minutes ≈ 815 hrs; issues log 836 rows / 85.4% closed; post-go-live burn-down 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4 (record).
  • % — Processing efficiency +15% (record, Sephora signed); robotics gate published at 98%, week two at 94, bots throttled to ~30% of volume, full volume week three (record).
  • Time — Council 5/15 → sign-off 7/31 → FSD 8/14; E2E 11/22 · 12/20 · 1/30; January and May go-lives; hypercare exited week 3 of 4 (record).
  • Customer — Zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives (record) — the constraint every other number was allowed to move inside.
  • Quality — Six cutover invariants held (Σ AFS conserved; no SKU sellable on 800 and 900 at once; negatives cleared first); rollback armed for both go-lives, never fired (record).

Against the goal stated above: one omni facility with self-transfers eliminated; zero customer-facing incidents through both go-lives; peak untouched; processing efficiency index 100 → 115 (+15%, record, Sephora signed) measured by DC Ops post-stabilization; robotics at full volume by week three. The trades were explicit: the comingle-reserve improvements went out of the release (a later enhancement, priced at steering), the throttle traded a week of robot volume for a root-caused ramp, and for four months every report had to declare which system of record it read — the interim's price.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

The 98% robotics gate was set from the pre-ramp baseline and the incident budget before the event — and when week two hit 94, it fired: I throttled bots to ~30% of volume rather than let the ramp erode the floor. A gate that cannot stop the program is decoration; mine did.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

S18 — the 0.5% reconciliation tolerance published with Finance before mock one — is this LP's backup, and S9's ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, held twice, is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is my current role — the January and May go-lives and the Locus ramp are the most recent record I have for this principle.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Processing efficiency +15% is the signed record and the point of one pool; zero customer-facing incidents was the constraint every other number had to move inside. Also important: the robotics gate holding at 94 vs 98 with full volume by week three, and the burn-down 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY revenue figure — the +15% is processing efficiency (units per labor-hour) against the pre-UDC baseline, Sephora-signed, and the ≈$1.6M/yr is my model on an illustrative 100K-units/week DC, not a revenue change.

Probing — I'm concerned about the labor number behind the modeled ≈$0.9M — tell me more.

The fifteen percent is measured; the headcount and the $28 loaded rate are my assumptions. Pull the DC's actual direct hours for the two quarters before and after and multiply by payroll — that turns the model into a record in one afternoon.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

A gate that cannot stop the program is decoration — mine did, at 94. Differently: staff the interim reporting workstream on day one, not month three.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

This is an FC launch/retrofit with automation on the critical path — publish the ramp gate and the throttle before the robots arrive, stage go-lives so each carries one defect class, and hold peak sacred. Zero-then-publish is the same discipline as never letting one unit be promisable from two nodes at once.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The steering charter asked for one omni facility, self-transfers eliminated, zero customer-facing incidents and peak untouched — all four delivered, with +15% efficiency measured by DC Ops post-stabilization and the robotics at full volume week three.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (15). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the systems-impacted table had a named owner and a written LOE for every row — a dozen core systems, 20+ touched — and the success criteria were signed by steering. The LOE landed 7/2.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I drafted them from the steering charter — one omni facility, self-transfers eliminated, zero customer-facing incidents, peak untouched — and steering (DC Ops, IT, Supply Chain Strategy) signed them in the council pack.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Two live truths during the interim, a robotics ramp with no stop condition, and a peak collision. Each had a trigger, a mitigation and an owner — the shadow monitor, the 98% gate with a throttle dial, and the freeze in the pack.

How did you handle a business owner who objected at the design review?

Digital worried a virtual Retail DC would blur their forecasting lens. I kept three planning identities over one physical location so each lens survived — the concern became the design, not an argument.

Why two go-lives?

So we never hunted two defect classes in the same week. January proved reserve rules against live demand while the floor was untouched; May left warehouse execution as the only remaining class.

Build & Test — How did you sequence work?

~30 build tracks under one log; stories slotted by business criticality × effort; per-environment workbooks (Dev 310, QA 255, Perf 200, Prod 221 rows) told each track where it could test. Blocker + Critical open = 0 was the gate.

How were UAT entrance criteria set and who could fail a build?

SIT contract defects closed, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod and perf environments ready. QA owned the gate; DC Ops confirmed in UAT because QA had already demoed to them during build.

Describe a bug bash — what came out of it?

The dated E2E days: Inbound 11/22, Dotcom 12/20, Retail 1/30 — every function ran the full flow in one room, every finding logged with severity and owner before we left. The Locus 3-day workshop worked the same way for the robotics contract.

Cutover — What made you say go — or no-go?

The burn-down, not the room: Blocker + Critical at zero, mock 3 inside the 0.5% tolerance (the discipline S18 established, reused here), runbook rehearsed, rollback armed. I would have said no-go on one open Blocker; I never had to.

What were your rollback triggers and did any fire?

Sync < 99% for four consecutive hours, or order-flow p95 > 2× budget for one hour — per-interface rollback behind them. Armed for both go-lives; never fired.

How big was the cutover runbook?

343 tasks, 48,885 task-minutes — about 815 hours — May 1 to Jul 1, each task with an owner, duration and predecessor, and step 4 (zero-then-publish) as the one irreversible instant.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over?

Blockers per day under two, sustained, and the post-go-live spike decaying 271 → 65 → 21 → 14 → 4. Exited week 3 of 4. A calendar exit declares victory over open defects.

Which wave did you hold and how did the business take it?

The Locus ramp at week 2: 94 against a published 98. I throttled to ~30% for a week. DC Ops took it well because the gate and the dial were theirs before the ramp — the rule fired, I didn't improvise.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

The comingle-reserve improvements — out of the release to protect zero incidents, priced as a later enhancement at steering. Escalated: the staged timeline and peak-freeze boundary as decisions; the Locus RTL slip the week it surfaced. I refused to compress UAT to absorb it.

Why 98%? · Why not one go-live? · Do differently?

98% was set from the pre-ramp baseline and the incident budget before the event — a gate that cannot stop the program is decoration; mine did, at 94. One go-live would mean hunting two defect classes in one week. Differently: staff the interim reporting workstream on day one, not month three — for four months every report had to declare which system of record it read.

Where this lands at Amazon: this is an FC launch/retrofit with automation on the critical path — publish the ramp gate and the throttle before the robots arrive, stage go-lives so each carries one defect class, and hold peak sacred. The zero-then-publish invariant is the same discipline as never letting one unit be promisable from two nodes at once.

Measure SKU-level on-hand on the last day of 801 vs the first stable month of 901; the release is whatever the difference actually is." "Split shipments — evidence?" — "The softest row. Parcels per dotcom order from the ship system before and after May, times the carrier's per-parcel cost. If the point didn't move, I drop the row and the total is about $1.1M."

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Conservatively, about one point six million a year: roughly nine hundred thousand of labor, because the same volume takes thirteen percent fewer hours at plus fifteen percent efficiency; about two hundred thousand a year of carry on the two point two million of safety stock one pool releases versus two; about four hundred fifty thousand from fewer split shipments; and about a hundred thousand from the DC no longer shipping to itself. The plus fifteen percent and the zero incidents are measured and signed by Sephora; every dollar figure is my model on an illustrative hundred-thousand-units-a-week DC, and I will tell you which input each one rests on."

60-second pitch

"Situation. Two DCs on a legacy WMS, no documentation, an eight-month deadline, ~$1.8M budget, a $20M ROI pipeline gated behind go-live — about $2.5M a month of slip; I had never run a WMS migration. Task. Full lifecycle, accountable to sponsor and Finance. Action. Discovery: wrote the success criteria in week one; learned the floor in month one. Plan & Design: interface inventory with an owner and a test cell per contract; the 0.5% reconciliation tolerance published with Finance before mock 1. Build & Test: three mocks — 7% (units of measure, location names, order-status vocabularies) → 1.8% (in-transit timing) → 0.3%. Cutover: a 72-hour in-transit freeze; the legacy system read-only for 30 days; ~100 of ~400 RF devices failed the conversion — I invoked the rehearsed paper-pick fallback rather than roll back, and told leadership the training criterion slips 1.5 weeks. Hypercare: exit week 3 at under two blockers a day. Lesson: single-point dependencies get a what-if plan regardless of size. Result. Primary metric: inventory reconciliation variance at cutover — 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% against the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance. $ impact: on time, ~3% under the ~$1.8M budget (~$54K), and the $20M ROI pipeline unblocked the next month — slip avoided, not banked."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Two DCs on a legacy WMS; zero inherited documentation; a hard eight-month deadline with a $20M ROI pipeline of four programs gated behind go-live (~$2.5M/month of slip); budget ~$1.8M; and I had never run a WMS migration. Inventory accuracy across two DCs could not wobble at cutover.

T — Own the full lifecycle — discovery charter, design sign-off, the ERP ↔ WMS ↔ 3PL ↔ carrier interface inventory, UAT, cutover, hypercare — accountable to the sponsor and Finance.

The goal, written in the charter in week one: on the date, inside a published reconciliation tolerance, zero accuracy incidents. The risks on the register: undocumented interface surprises, conversion mismatch above tolerance, and 3PL/carrier freeze non-compliance — each with a trigger, a data-first mitigation and an owner; the one I missed: RF conversion sat on the log as a task, not a risk.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: conversion mismatch — gated at the 0.5% tolerance signed with Finance before mock 1, with mock 1 opening at 7%; accuracy incidents — zero allowed; the date — eight months out, hard; the budget — ~$1.8M.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (team 14 core + 6 DC Ops; month markers illustrative unless stated):

  1. Discovery (month 1) — sponsor charter written in week one (objectives, assumptions, success criteria); phase entrance/exit criteria; interface inventory v0 from message logs; the systems-impacted table with LOE per owner. Exit bar: charter signed by sponsor and Finance; every interface has a named owner; I could walk receive → pick → ship unaided.
  2. Plan & Design (months 2–3) — design sign-off pack; interface inventory v1 with a test cell per contract; the conversion approach (masters → open orders → on-hand → in-transit as its own batch); the mapping-table standard (data, not code); the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock 1. Exit bar: council sign-off; tolerance signed; stories slotted by business criticality × effort.
  3. Build & Test (months 4–7) — build tracking; the QA demo schedule to DC Ops; the UAT entrance memo (function testing done, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod + perf ready); three mock cutovers with written root causes; after the RF miss, the training plan and device readiness table. Exit bar: mock 3 ≤ 0.5% (0.3%) → written go-live recommendation; training sign-off (slipped 1.5 weeks, date held).
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (month 8) — the cutover runbook; the 72-hour transfer freeze; conversion batches; post-load reconciliation; the legacy read-only switch; the go/no-go call. When ~100 of ~400 RF devices failed the conversion script, I invoked the rehearsed paper-pick fallback rather than roll back. Exit bar: post-load mismatch inside tolerance; ship confirms posting; zero accuracy incidents.
  5. Hypercare / Close (4 weeks planned) — blockers/day read daily; the 30-day failback watch; budget closed; the exit memo with the single-point-dependency lesson. Exit bar: <2 blockers/day sustained → exit week 3; ~3% under budget; pipeline programs started the next month.

I wrote the charter in week one and learned the floor and the vendor docs in month one; the team was 14 core plus 6 DC Ops, with Finance co-owning the tolerance. Mine: the charter, the entrance/exit criteria, the interface inventory, the conversion approach, the mapping-table standard (data, not code), the tolerance, the go/no-go recommendation and the fallback call; the team built, converted, tested and ran the floor. The contribution: writing the success criteria before anyone asked — the date, the tolerance, zero accuracy incidents, the numeric hypercare exit — and the three-mock discipline read by cause class: 7% → 1.8% → 0.3%, each mock removing a named defect category.

The obstacles: no documentation — the interface inventory was built from message logs, every interface with a named owner and a test cell; my own inexperience — the floor walked unaided by the end of month one; and ~100 of ~400 RF devices failing at cutover — answered by the rehearsed paper-pick fallback, not a rollback, with the training criterion slipping 1.5 weeks while the date held. Stories were slotted by business criticality × effort, with UAT entrance criteria written in the charter — function testing done, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod and perf ready — QA could fail a build, and DC Ops confirmed because QA had demoed during build; buy-in: sponsor and Finance signed the charter week one, so nobody negotiated the exit later. The decision challenged was rolling back when the RF devices failed — challenged because inventory was inside tolerance and the failure was device conversion, not data: the rehearsed fallback fired instead, and leadership heard it the same day with the slip priced and my name on the cause. The RF miss is mine: I scored the task by effort when impact was what mattered.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: mock variance 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% vs the 0.5% tolerance; on time; ~3% under ~$1.8M (~$54K); the $20M pipeline unblocked the next month.

  • $ — ~3% under the ~$1.8M budget (~$54K, record); the $20M ROI pipeline unblocked the next month — ~$2.5M/month of slip avoided, not banked; ≈$45K modeled paper-pick fallback cost paid deliberately to hold the date.
  • # — ~100 of ~400 RF devices failed the conversion script — the rehearsed paper-pick fallback fired; team 14 core + 6 DC Ops across two DCs (record).
  • % — Mock variance 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% against the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock 1 (record).
  • Time — Eight-month deadline held; 72-hour in-transit freeze; legacy read-only for 30 days; training slipped 1.5 weeks while the date did not move; hypercare exited week 3 of 4 (record).
  • Customer — Zero accuracy incidents at cutover; ship confirms posting; post-load mismatch inside tolerance (record exit bars).
  • Quality — Mocks read by cause class, each removing a named defect category — units of measure, location names, order-status vocabularies, then in-transit timing; failback rehearsed, never used (record).

Against the goal stated above: on the date; 0.3% against the 0.5% tolerance published with Finance before mock 1; zero accuracy incidents; ~3% under the ~$1.8M budget; the $20M pipeline unblocked the next month. The trades: sequence, not scope — training slipped 1.5 weeks so the go-live date held, told to leadership the same day with the slip priced — and ≈$45K of modeled paper-pick productivity paid deliberately to protect the critical path.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

Because the standard was published before it could be met: the 0.5% tolerance signed with Finance before mock 1, so the number belonged to Finance and me before the test. Mock 1's 7% was the price; mock 3's 0.3% was worth it — and the tolerance was never renegotiated.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Insist on the Highest Standards?

S2 — the robotics gate held at 94 vs 98 — is this LP's primary; S9's ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, held twice, is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is a Sephora record, and its discipline is what the current UDC program reuses — S2's three mock cutovers run against the same 0.5% tolerance. S2 is the most recent same-LP record.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

The mock trajectory against the published tolerance — 7% → 1.8% → 0.3% vs 0.5% — is the record that made “go” a written recommendation rather than confidence in the room; on time, ~3% under budget and the $20M pipeline unblocked the next month are what the sponsor and Finance measured.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the ~$54K is ~3% under the ~$1.8M budget, and the ~$2.5M/month is slip exposure avoided, not banked; the pipeline's value belonged to the four programs it gated.

Probing — I'm concerned about the $875K avoided-slip figure — tell me more.

It is record arithmetic on a slip that did not happen, so I never bank it — it is the price that made the fallback the obvious call. What I'd measure is the pipeline's first program start date against plan, which was the next month.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Any single-point dependency in front of an exit criterion gets a what-if plan, regardless of estimated effort. Differently: dual-path device conversion on day one, and a device model × OS × script pass/fail table before training week.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Cutover discipline in its most literal form — a tolerance published with Finance before the first rehearsal, mocks read by cause class rather than headline, a freeze, a tested failback, and a numeric hypercare exit nobody can negotiate.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The charter asked for the date, the tolerance and zero accuracy incidents — delivered on time, 0.3% against 0.5%, zero accuracy incidents, ~3% under budget, and hypercare exited week 3 of 4 under the charter's own blockers-per-day number.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — You had never run a WMS migration; how did you know discovery was done?

When I could walk receive → put-away → pick → ship on the floor unaided and every interface I saw in the message logs had a row, an owner and a test cell. That took month one; the charter had been written week one.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote them in week one — on the date, inside a published reconciliation tolerance, zero accuracy incidents, hypercare exit under two blockers a day — and the sponsor and Finance signed the charter.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Undocumented interface surprises, conversion mismatch above tolerance, and 3PL/carrier freeze non-compliance — each with a trigger, a data-first mitigation and an owner. RF conversion was on the log as a task, not a risk; that was my miss.

Why publish the tolerance before you knew you could hit it?

So the number belonged to Finance and me before the test, not after. Mock 1's 7% was the price; mock 3's 0.3% was worth it.

Build & Test — How were UAT entrance criteria set and who could fail a build?

Function testing done, conversion build done, test data handed off, pre-prod and perf ready — written in the charter. QA could fail a build; DC Ops confirmed in UAT because QA had demoed to them during build.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The three mocks. Everyone in one room reading the mismatch by cause class — units of measure, location drift, status vocabularies, then in-transit timing. Nothing left the room without a class, an owner and a data fix.

How did you read a mock?

Never by the headline number alone. Each delta got a root-cause class; if the total shrank without a named cause, the mock was not evidence. 7 → 1.8 → 0.3 each removed a category.

Cutover — What made you say go?

A written recommendation off mock 3 at 0.3% against 0.5%, the freeze agreed in writing, failback tested, training signed off — 1.5 weeks late, inside the window.

What were your rollback triggers and did any fire?

Post-go-live mismatch above 0.5% for two days, or ship-confirm posting failing beyond two hours — rollback per interface, the legacy system read-only for 30 days as the failback. Rehearsed; never used. The paper-pick contingency, also rehearsed, did fire — for the RF miss.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over?

Blockers per day under two, sustained — exited week 3 of 4. The number was in the charter before go-live, so nobody negotiated the exit.

What did you hand over at hypercare exit?

The interface inventory with owners and test cells, the mapping tables owned by DC Ops and ERP, the failback plan still armed for the rest of the 30 days, and the device readiness table. The RF lesson went into the sponsor read-out with my name on it.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

Nowhere on scope — I traded sequence: training slipped 1.5 weeks so the go-live date held, and I told leadership the same day with the slip priced. Escalated the RF miss as a priced decision with my name on the cause. The rule I left: any single-point dependency in front of an exit criterion gets a what-if plan, regardless of size.

You'd never run a WMS migration — why you? · Do differently?

I wrote the success criteria before anyone asked and learned the floor and the vendor docs in month one; the mocks were where I proved it. Differently: dual-path device conversion on day one; a device model × OS × script pass/fail table before training week.

Where this lands at Amazon: the cutover discipline in its most literal form — a tolerance published with Finance before the first rehearsal, mocks read by cause class rather than headline, a freeze, a tested failback, and a numeric hypercare exit nobody can negotiate. The RF miss is the honest beat: a rehearsed contingency fired, the date held, and the lesson became a rule.

|    +- cutover variance 7% -> 1.8% -> 0.3% vs 0.5% tolerance

       critical path protected -> pipeline on time

If challenged. "Why not roll back when the RF devices failed?" — "Because the fallback was rehearsed and the rollback wasn't needed: inventory was inside tolerance; the failure was device conversion, not data. I paid ~$45K of paper-picking to protect ~$2.5M a month of pipeline — and took the miss upstairs the same afternoon with my name on the cause."
