S11 and S12 are flagged super-important for this loop: the WBR-and-waves machine and the roadmap flip are the two Flexport records most likely to carry Deliver Results, Think Big and Frugality. S16 is the Ownership/failure-adjacent companion.

S11 — Flexport: Shipment Visibility launch — 7 → 68 behind graduation gates, the WBR, and pre-prod measurement

Amazon LPs: Deliver Results (primary) · Ownership · Customer Obsession (graduation criteria per wave) · Earn Trust (the discarded 99.4% week) · Hire and Develop (backup — wave teams grown into owners). Crosswalk: loop packet MAIN + S18 + S26 — carries the §1.3 conflicts (automation 40→90 here vs 60→97 there; WBR hours 1.5–2 → ~4 here vs 30→6 there). Resolve before the loop; inside this packet, this page is canon. Never "10×" — say 7 → 68 (9.7×). Never "$1.00 → $0.03" or "70% baseline."

Honesty label. Program owner across nine teams / ~100 people; the WBR format was copied from the LSA review, not invented; week counts for the build are illustrative; the ~$1.5M pre-prod saving is a separate canonical figure, never merged with CTS.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Flexport's visibility product had public targets — 68 monthly active customers, 170 MAU, 32K shipments — off seven pilots at $0.66 a container; Sales wanted the whole pool boarded at once, and leadership said the program was not showing value. Task. As program owner across nine teams / ~100 people, I owned the boarding path, the measurement system, and the pre-prod proof. Action. Discovery: ~50-container calibration entry bar per client; WBR input/output OS copied from an existing review, seven metrics frozen. Plan & Design: six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1; launch gate 100 real containers × 7 checks; sample size 300 containers, ±1.6% at 95% confidence. Build & Test: weekly ops audit; WBR by hand 1.5–2 then ~4 hrs/wk; pre-prod ingest, per-hop monitors, kill switches. Cutover: wave 1 (2 clients) → wave 2 (4 of 7) → wave 3 (20+) held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss from one bad source → full rollout. Hypercare: discarded a flattering 99.4% three-touch week; the VP unlocked the 68-client rollout two months early off the WBR. Lesson: board no faster than automation matures; a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion. Result. Primary metric: cost-to-serve per container — $0.66 → $0.03 (−95.5%) across customers 7 → 68 (9.7×) with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount; automation 40% → 90%; the ~$1.5M pre-prod saving is a separate record figure."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Senior leadership told me the visibility program wasn't demonstrating value at the anticipated rate — it would drop from #3 to #14 of 16 initiatives.

T — I had been reporting activity, not evidence. Funding and the 68-customer rollout hung on whether value was legible.

The public targets: 68 monthly active customers, 170 MAU, 32K shipments, off seven pilots at $0.66 a container — and I was program owner across nine teams / ~100 people, owning the boarding path, the measurement system and the pre-prod proof, with Sales wanting the whole pool boarded at once. The risks: boarding faster than automation matured — a bad first impression at scale, with rollbacks — and the program losing its funding slot; the critique was about evidence, not effort, and I accepted that first.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: customers — 7 pilots; target 68. Cost-to-serve — $0.66 a container. Automation — 40%; ETA accuracy — 60%. All of it to move with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (canonical: WBR copied from the LSA review — Mon–Sun week, five-week window, WoW = (B−A)/A, ±5% green/red, every input tagged to an output; seven frozen metrics; run by hand 1.5–2 hrs/wk then ~4 for over a year; wave roster W1 Q4'21 (2 clients) → W2 Q1'22 (4 of a 7-pool) → W3 Q2'22 (20+, held on ETA accuracy) → full H2'22):

  1. Discovery — wrote the honest diagnosis (reporting activity, not evidence); inventoried what leadership actually decides on (rollout pace, funding); chose the LSA WBR as the pattern to copy rather than invent. Exit bar: leadership agrees on the questions the review must answer weekly.
  2. Plan & Design — froze seven metric definitions (coverage auto, coverage w/ human, tickets, tickets per active container, 4PL SLA %, 3PL SLA %, delta); input→output tagging; the ±5% WoW rule; the 15-day inactive rule as the honest denominator; the four-system pull procedure; six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1. Exit bar: definitions signed by the owners of each input; first Monday deck dry-run.
  3. Build & Test — built the deck manually every week (1.5–2 hrs → ~4); pre-decomposed every red with an owner and a dated fix; enforced the data-honesty rule (a truncated week is a data miss, not a result); pre-prod: 300-container measurement, per-hop monitors, kill switches. Exit bar: two consecutive Mondays where every red arrives with an owner and the number verifies the following week.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — the Monday WBR became the operating forum, with the rollout decision taken in the review; waves boarded only through the six graduation criteria; wave 3 held in public on ETA accuracy traced to one bad source. Exit bar: VP unlocks the 68-client rollout two months early.
  5. Hypercare / Close — handed the manual pull to the Snowflake pipeline (S16) with the definitions in code; left the critic steering by the numbers. Exit bar: automation 40% → 90%; CTS $0.66 → $0.03; the same critic uses the review to steer.

The WBR format was copied from the LSA review, not invented — copying a trusted format was the point — and I ran it by hand every Monday for over a year, 1.5–2 hours and then about 4. Mine: the honest diagnosis, the frozen definitions, the WBR built by hand every week, the graduation criteria, the hold decisions and the pre-prod measurement design; nine teams built and operated, each input owner signed the definition they would be red against, and the VP took the rollout decisions in the review. The contribution was making value legible: seven frozen metric definitions signed by their owners, input→output tagging, and the data-honesty rule — including discarding a flattering truncated 99.4% week and printing a data miss instead.

The obstacles: Sales wanting the whole pool boarded at once — answered with six graduation criteria published before wave 1, and wave 3 held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss traced to one bad source — the rule fired, and the held customers became the cleanest wave; bus factor one on the pull — retired by handing it to the Snowflake pipeline (S16). The priority rule was board no faster than automation matures — waves only through the six criteria, every red pre-decomposed with an owner and a dated fix; buy-in came from leadership agreeing the questions the review must answer weekly, and the Monday WBR becoming the operating forum where the rollout decision was taken. The decisions challenged: the “board everything now” push — met with published gates — and the temptation to print the 99.4% week: three touches is not a week, and a number that cannot survive the next Monday teaches the VP the wrong thing.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: CTS $0.66 → $0.03 (−95.5%); customers 7 → 68 (9.7×), zero rollbacks; accuracy 60% → 90%; both public targets two months early.

  • $ — CTS $0.66 → $0.03 per container (record); the ~$1.5M pre-prod measurement saving is a separate record figure, never merged with CTS; direct CTS dollars ≈$50K/yr modeled on an illustrative container count.
  • # — Customers 7 → 68 (9.7×) with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount; targets 68 customers / 170 MAU / 32K shipments (record).
  • % — CTS −95.5%; automation 40% → 90%; accuracy 60% → 90% (record).
  • Time — Both public targets hit two months early; the rollout unlocked two months early off the WBR (record).
  • Customer — Six graduation criteria published to Sales before wave 1 — including CSAT ≥ consignee average; wave 3 held in public on an ETA-accuracy miss, and the held customers became the cleanest wave (record).
  • Quality — A flattering truncated 99.4% week discarded and printed as a data miss; seven metric definitions frozen and later moved into code (record).

Against the goal stated above: customers 7 → 68 (9.7×) with zero rollbacks and no added ops headcount; CTS $0.66 → $0.03 (−95.5%); automation 40 → 90%; accuracy 60 → 90%; both public targets hit two months early — and the VP unlocked the 68-client rollout off the review's own numbers. The trades: new metrics were parked to keep the seven stable, and boarding speed was traded for a rollout that never rolled back — wave 3 held publicly rather than take a bad first impression at scale.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Deliver Results?

Because both public targets landed two months early with zero rollbacks: CTS $0.66 → $0.03 across customers 7 → 68 on the same ops team. Results delivered behind graduation gates, not by boarding faster than the automation could carry.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Deliver Results?

S18 — 8 months, both hubs, gate cleared — is the backup; S2's comingle launch and S6's three-months-to-live are the thirds.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Flexport — recent, though not current role. The current-role Deliver Results record is S2: the comingle launch, zero incidents, +15% signed.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

CTS −95.5% across 7 → 68 with zero rollbacks is the unit-economics proof that scale came without headcount; automation 40 → 90% is the mechanism; two months early is the time result. The ~$1.5M pre-prod saving matters too — kept separate because it is a separate record.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the $0.66 → $0.03 is cost-to-serve per container, before vs after, and the direct dollars are honestly small: ≈$50K/yr modeled on an illustrative container count. The value is scale without headcount, and the ~$1.5M pre-prod figure is a separate record I never merge with CTS.

Probing — I'm concerned about your containers-per-shipment assumption — tell me more.

It is a guess and I said so — the record gives me thirty-two thousand shipments, not containers; at one and a half to four containers per shipment the saving runs thirty to eighty thousand. I would firm it up by pulling the active-container count straight from the 15-day active rule in the WBR.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Board no faster than automation matures; a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion. Differently: start the WBR at kickoff, not after the critique.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

A WBR culture story told to the company that invented the WBR — copy the trusted format, freeze definitions, tag inputs to outputs, and let the review make the rollout decision; the graduation criteria are Amazon-shaped launch gates, published before the wave and held in public.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The public targets were 68 customers, 170 MAU and 32K shipments — both targets hit two months early, 7 → 68 with zero rollbacks, automation 40 → 90%, and the same critic who said value wasn't legible steering by the review.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When leadership had told me what they actually decide on — rollout pace and funding — and agreed the questions the review must answer weekly. Their critique was about evidence, not effort, and I accepted that first.

Why copy a format instead of designing one?

The LSA review already had leadership's trust — five-week window, WoW at ±5%, inputs tagged to outputs. Inventing a format would have spent credibility I didn't have.

Plan & Design — Who wrote the metric definitions and who signed?

I wrote the seven; the owner of each input — Data Services, Support, GTM — signed the definition they would be red against. Frozen after that; new metrics out of scope.

What was in your risk register?

Measurement-caused reds, a flattering wrong number, and bus factor one. Mitigations: decompose before the meeting, print data misses honestly, automate the pull — which became S16.

How did you handle the objection to freezing the list?

Stability over coverage — a number that changes definition can't steer a rollout. I offered a parking lot for new metrics and kept the seven.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a red week?

Decomposed before the meeting, an owner named, a dated fix, and the same number verified the following Monday. A red without an owner was my miss, not theirs.

Describe a hard call in the build.

Discarding a truncated 99.4% week. It flattered the program and would have taught the VP a number that couldn't survive the next Monday. I printed a data miss.

Cutover — When did the review become "live"?

The Monday the VP took the rollout decision in the room — unlocking the 68-client rollout two months early off the numbers, not a narrative.

Which wave did the review hold and how did Sales take it?

Wave 3, on ETA accuracy traced to one bad source. Sales had the six graduation criteria before wave 1, so the hold was the rule firing — and the customers we held became the cleanest wave.

Hypercare — How did you know the review would outlive your hands?

When the pipeline (S16) produced the same seven numbers within 1% for consecutive weeks and the critic who started this steered by them.

What did you keep doing after the review was credible?

Running it by hand every Monday for over a year — 1.5–2 hours, then about 4 — until the pipeline matched within 1%. A review that skips a week teaches leadership it can be skipped.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

New metrics for stability, and boarding speed for a rollout that never rolled back — held wave 3 publicly rather than take a bad first impression at scale. Escalated nothing outside the review — the review was the escalation forum, with the VP deciding in it every Monday.

Why discard 99.4%? · Do differently?

Three touches is not a week; printing it would have taught the VP a number that couldn't survive the next Monday. Differently: start the WBR at kickoff, not after the critique.

Where this lands at Amazon: this is a WBR culture story told to the company that invented the WBR — the respect move is that I copied a trusted format rather than inventing one, froze definitions, tagged inputs to outputs, and let the review make the rollout decision. The graduation criteria are Amazon-shaped launch gates: published before the wave, held in public when they fired.

If challenged. "The CTS saving is only $50K?" — "Directly, yes — and I say that out loud. The point of −95.5% was never the direct dollars; it's that 7 customers became 68 with zero added ops headcount. The modeled figure is honest because the volume is; the scaling is the record." "Why ±1.6%?" — "n = 300 at an expected 98% accuracy gives a 95% confidence interval of about ±1.6 points — big enough to certify the gate, small enough to afford. That measurement path cut rollout from 6 months to 1.5 and carries the separate ~$1.5M record."

Total impact statement — say it like this

"Honestly, the cost-to-serve number is a unit-economics result, not a big dollar number: ninety-five percent off per container is measured, and at the thirty-two-thousand-shipment target that is only about fifty thousand dollars a year of direct cost, which is my model on an illustrative container count. What it bought is scale: sixty-eight customers boarded on the same ops team with zero rollbacks, about two and a half people we never had to hire, and a rollout unlocked two months early, which I model at roughly a quarter million of one-time revenue pull-forward. If challenged — the full answers.

Your containers-per-shipment is a guess.

It is, and I said so. The record gives me thirty-two thousand shipments, not containers; I used two and a half FCL containers per shipment, and at one and a half to four the saving runs thirty to eighty thousand. I would firm it up by pulling the active-container count straight from the fifteen-day active rule in the WBR, which is the same denominator the review already used.

Fifty thousand a year is small — why lead with it?

Because it is honest, and because the point was never the dollars, it was boarding nine point seven times the customers without nine point seven times the ops headcount. The hardest part of that claim is the one-minute-per-interaction assumption behind the two-and-a-half FTE; I would measure it from the ticket-grain automation data, five hundred thirty-eight auto versus four hundred fifty-six human.

Where does the million and a half come from, then?

That is the pre-prod measurement path, a separate canonical: three hundred containers at plus or minus one point six percent let us prove the product in six weeks instead of six months. It is a record figure, it is not cost-to-serve, and I never add it to the fifty thousand. To firm it up I would want the finance sign-off memo that priced the four and a half months saved.

60-second pitch

"Situation. The Freight Forwarding App export roadmap was operator-first, 18 months, already socialized, and my manager owned it; baselines 339.5 min/shipment and 2,200 operator interactions per departed shipment. Task. With no authority over the roadmap, re-sequence it on cost-to-serve evidence. Action. Discovery: time study × loaded rates. SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The inherited launch plan for the freight-forwarding app was operator-persona first, 18 months, already socialized, and my manager owned it. Baselines: 339.5 min/shipment time-to-serve, 2,200 operator interactions per departed shipment.

T — I owned the sequencing recommendation — and changing a plan with my manager's name on it.

The real question was time-to-value and unit cost, not persona preference. The challenges: re-opening a committed, socialized plan; getting honest re-estimates with confidence levels; and not abandoning operators while partners went first. Left as inherited, the plan meant eighteen months to first value on the operator path, with operators still spending 20% of their time in the old app at the end of it — half the unit saving (−25% vs −50% at the work-item grain), reached a year later.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: time-to-serve — 339.5 minutes per shipment; operator interactions — 2,200 per departed shipment; time to first value — month 18 on the inherited path; cost-to-serve at the work-item grain — the −25% vs −50% comparison the recommendation would turn on.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery — baseline memo (339.5 min/shipment; 2,200 interactions); bare-minimum scope per persona cut with the business; the question restated as time-to-value and unit cost, not persona preference. Exit bar: business agrees the bare minimums; engineering agrees to re-estimate with confidence levels.
  2. Plan & Design — re-estimates (9 months operator vs 6 months partner) with confidence buffers (×1.0/×1.4 + 15%); the 20%-still-in-old-app finding; work-item-level cost-to-serve translation (−25% vs −50%); the rebuild-vs-reuse rule per module (>8 dev-weeks → reuse); read privately to my manager first; engineering got two days to break it. Exit bar: my manager puts his name on the flip at the Bellevue onsite; sequencing signed.
  3. Build & Test — 15 domains × 6 modules (Booking Validated → Customs Declaration → …); Jira burn-up with the scope tripwire ("new work estimated and swapped, never absorbed"); operator-interactions-eliminated as the success metric; the tenet "no go-live without every work item assigned to one operator." Exit bar: burn-up 55.8% → 83.2% across Q3–Q4; every work item owned.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (waves) — MVP with 11 partners in 10 countries; partner migration plan published; operators' two further quarters on old screens priced and published. Exit bar: 48 of 53 import partners migrated within a month; MVP live.
  5. Hypercare / Close — tracked interactions eliminated; sequenced FCL 2022 → Air/LCL 2023; close-out with ~$5M cost avoidance and 66% less effort. Exit bar: value in 6 months instead of 18; operators moved onto the shared chassis on the published schedule.

Engineering produced the re-estimates; the cost-avoidance figure is the record's, said once; the run-rate decomposition is my model. Mine specifically: the baseline memo, the comparison model, the rebuild/reuse rule and the scope tripwire; the business signed the bare minimums per persona, and my manager signed the sequencing and owned the flip. The contribution: restating the question as time-to-value and unit cost, building the work-item-grain comparison, and reading it privately to my manager first so the flip could carry his name at the Bellevue onsite — engineering got two days to break it.

The obstacle was changing a plan with my manager's name on it — handled privately first, with the numbers: 9 versus 6 months, 20% still in the old app, −25% versus −50%; he argued two points, I adjusted one, and the onsite ratified it. Optimistic estimates got confidence buffers, operators got a priced, published two-quarter schedule, and silent scope absorption got the burn-up tripwire. Priorities were ranked by benefit × impact divided by dev-weeks per module, with new work estimated and swapped, never absorbed; buy-in ran private-first, two days for engineering to break the model, then the room ratifies — I have never won a disagreement by seniority. The decision challenged was my manager's own operator-first sequencing — on cost-to-serve evidence at the work-item grain, not persona preference — and the influence mechanism was making the better decision his decision, with his name on it at the onsite.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: effort to first value −66%; value month 6 instead of 18. ~$5M cost avoidance (record, said once); CTS −50% vs −25%; 48/53 partners in a month.

  • $ — ~$5M cost avoidance (record, said once — never “avoidance” and “annual reduction” as two results); the ≈$1.5M/yr run-rate underneath is my model at −50% CTS on an illustrative 20K export shipments.
  • # — 48 of 53 import partners migrated within a month; MVP with 11 partners in 10 countries; baselines 339.5 min/shipment and 2,200 operator interactions per departed shipment (record).
  • % — Engineering effort to first value −66%; CTS −50% partner-first vs −25% operator-first at the work-item grain; burn-up 55.8% → 83.2% across two quarters (record).
  • Time — Value in month 6 instead of month 18 (record); FCL 2022 → Air/LCL 2023 sequenced off the interactions read.
  • Customer — Partners completed end-to-end in the new app; operators' two further quarters on old screens were priced and published, not hidden (record).
  • Quality — New scope estimated and swapped, never absorbed; the tenet — no go-live without every work item assigned to one operator (record).

Against the goal stated above: first value in month 6 instead of month 18, at 66% less engineering effort; −50% versus −25% cost-to-serve at the work-item grain against the stated baselines; 48 of 53 partners migrated within a month. The trades were priced and published: operators stayed on old screens two more quarters so partners finished end-to-end in six months, and estimates carried confidence buffers — a slower, honest plan over a fast optimistic one.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Think Big?

Because the flip re-opened a committed 18-month plan on evidence and changed the destination: value in month 6 instead of 18 at 66% less engineering effort — the goal mattered more than the plan. The reversal trigger and the scope tripwire are what made the big swing safe.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Think Big?

S9 — the 1,000-lane vision, staged honestly — is the backup; S1's book-of-record migration is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Flexport — recent. For a current-role Think Big record, S1 — the book-of-record migration — is the one I would pair with it.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Effort −66% and value in month 6 instead of 18 are the record pair that justified the flip; the cost avoidance is the program's signed figure, said once. 48 of 53 partners migrating within a month proved the partner path was real.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the avoidance is one-time (engineering never spent, plus a year of unit savings pulled forward), and I never present it as an annual reduction; the recurring run-rate underneath is a separate, smaller modeled number on an illustrative shipment volume.

Probing — I'm concerned about the engineer count and rate behind your decomposition — tell me more.

Twelve engineers at $20K per month are my numbers — the record does not itemize the avoidance. At ten to fifteen engineers and fifteen to twenty-five thousand the effort piece runs $1.8M to $4.5M, and the record's figure sits inside that band; I would firm it up with the two scoping envelopes — 66.2 and 99.8 engineer-months — and Finance's actual loaded rate.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Bring the model, name the owner, write the reversal trigger. The scope tripwire — new work estimated and swapped, never absorbed — is what kept the burn-up honest, 55.8% → 83.2%.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Think Big with Frugality attached — re-open a committed plan when the goal matters more than the plan, price it in unit economics, and have the owner whose name was on the original ratify the change; the reversal trigger and the scope tripwire make the swing safe.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The inherited plan promised operator value in 18 months; the flip delivered first value in month 6 at 66% less effort, −50% versus −25% cost-to-serve at the work-item grain, and 48 of 53 partners migrated within a month.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When each persona had a bare-minimum scope the business had signed and engineering had agreed to re-estimate with confidence levels. Baselines — 339.5 minutes and 2,200 interactions per shipment — were on paper first.

Who wrote the success criteria?

I did: time-to-value and cost-to-serve at work-item level, with monthly operator interactions eliminated as the metric. The business signed the minimums; my manager signed the sequencing.

Plan & Design — How did you handle your manager's plan being wrong?

Privately first, with the numbers — 9 versus 6 months, 20% still in the old app, −25% versus −50%. He argued two points, I adjusted one, and the onsite ratified it with his name on it.

What was in your risk register at ratification?

Optimistic estimates, operators feeling abandoned, and scope absorbed silently. Mitigations: confidence buffers, a priced two-quarter schedule, and the burn-up tripwire.

What was the rebuild-vs-reuse rule?

Over eight dev-weeks to rebuild — reuse; under — rebuild; rank by benefit × impact divided by dev-weeks. Agreed with product, UX and engineering per module.

Build & Test — How did you sequence in Jira?

Fifteen domains by six modules, tracked as a burn-up against planned engineering days — 55.8% to 83.2% across two quarters. New work was estimated and swapped, never absorbed.

What was your definition of done for a module?

Every work item assigned to one operator and its interactions-eliminated count recorded. The tenet: no go-live without every work item owned.

Cutover — What did each wave deliver?

MVP: 11 partners in 10 countries end-to-end on the new backend. Migration: 48 of 53 import partners within a month. Then operators onto the same chassis, then Air/LCL in 2023.

What was your rollback?

Partners could keep working in the old flow during migration, and operators were still on old screens by design. Reversibility was built into the sequencing.

Hypercare — How did you know the flip was right?

Value in six months instead of eighteen, 66% less engineering effort, about $5M cost avoidance — said once — and operators moved onto the chassis on the published schedule.

What did you track after the partners were live?

Monthly operator interactions eliminated against the 2,200-per-shipment baseline, by work item, and the burn-up on the remaining modules. Air/LCL 2023 was scheduled off that read, not off a date on a slide.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

Operators stayed on old screens two more quarters so partners finished end-to-end in six months — priced, published, scored. Escalated nothing past my manager. I have never won a disagreement by seniority — I rank, privately first, and let the room ratify.

How did your manager react?

He asked for the estimation details, argued two points, and put his name on the flip at the onsite. That's the point of private-first.

Where this lands at Amazon: Think Big with Frugality attached — re-opening a committed plan because the goal mattered more than the plan, priced in unit economics, ratified by the owner whose name was on the original. The reversal trigger and the scope tripwire are the mechanisms that made a big swing safe.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"About five million of cost avoidance, and I say that once. Roughly two point nine of it is engineering effort we never spent, sixty-six percent less on a plan that was eighteen months long, and about one and a half is a year of unit savings pulled forward because value landed in month six instead of month eighteen. Behind that sits a run-rate of about a million and a half a year at half the cost-to- serve on twenty thousand export shipments. The sixty-six percent, the six versus eighteen months, and the minus fifty versus minus twenty-five are the record; the five million is the program's signed figure; the split between engineering and pulled-forward value, and the shipment volume, are my model."

If challenged — the full answers.

Twelve engineers and twenty thousand dollars a month are your numbers, not the program's.

Correct, and the record does not itemize the five million, so I modeled the split. Ten to fifteen engineers at fifteen to twenty-five thousand puts the effort piece anywhere from one point eight to four and a half million; the record's five million sits inside that band. I would firm it up with the two scoping envelopes, sixty-six point two and ninety-nine point eight engineer-months, and finance's actual loaded rate.

Where do twenty thousand shipments come from?

It is an illustrative FCL export volume, and it is the softest input: at fifteen to thirty thousand the run-rate moves from one point one to two point three million a year. The three-thirty-nine-and-a-half minutes and the per-minute rates are the time study; I would replace the volume with the departed-shipment count from the same system that produced the two thousand two hundred interactions baseline.

Are you double counting the pulled-forward value and the five million?

No. The five million is the total; the pulled- forward year is one slice of how it decomposes, and the million and a half a year is the run-rate underneath, not a second result. To make that airtight I would ask for the finance close-out that priced the avoidance and show the two slices summing to it.

S16 — Flexport: four systems into one Snowflake pipeline — and the security-review miss I owned upward the day it surfaced

Amazon LPs: Ownership (primary — bus factor 1, and owning the miss) · Invent and Simplify (backup) · Frugality (the >$100K priced baseline) · Learn and Be Curious. Crosswalk: loop packet MAIN's BRD/pipeline beat + the security-review miss — consistent. Same program family as S11 — never both to one interviewer.

Honesty label. I wrote the BRD and owned the cutover; the infra team owned the build. The >$100K/yr is a priced baseline in the infra team's own loaded rates — priced, not claimed as savings. The two-week security-review miss is mine, owned upward the same day.
60-second pitch

"Situation. The WBR ran on four systems stitched in Sheets by me — bus factor 1, accuracy 60%, tickets with no container ID. Task. I wrote the BRD and owned the cutover; infra owned the build. Action. Discovery: froze seven metric definitions; priced the status quo at >$100K/yr in infra's own rates. Plan & Design: ETL → S3 → SQS → Snowflake → Looker; parallel-run gate within 1%. Build & Test: a cross-reference table first, then a real join key pushed upstream. Cutover: consecutive weeks inside 1%. Hypercare: my miss — I did not plan the data-access security review; it cost two weeks; I owned it upward the same day; zero review deliveries skipped. Lesson: every kickoff asks whose approval the data needs and its lead time. Result. Primary metric: WBR data accuracy — 60% → 90% once four systems became one Snowflake pipeline. $ impact: the manual status quo was priced at >$100K/yr (a priced baseline, not a claimed saving); first automated catch was a 4% ticket-sync drop."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The Monday review ran on four systems that didn't talk, stitched in Google Sheets by one person — me: bus factor 1, accuracy 60%, 1.5–2 hours per snapshot, tickets carried no container ID. A VP was steering on a number one person could break.

T — Nobody had written requirements for a program's operating metrics. I wrote the BRD and owned the cutover; the infra team owned the build.

The goal: one pipeline producing the same seven numbers, accurate and owned by more than one human. The challenges: the ticket join gap, earning parity before anyone would switch, and — as it surfaced — a data-access security review I had not planned. The risk of leaving it: a wrong number steering the review, and silent data loss nobody would catch — the 4% ticket-sync drop was invisible in the manual era.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: WBR accuracy — 60%; bus factor — 1; snapshot effort — 1.5–2 hours per Monday; ticket-to-container joinability — none.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (week counts illustrative except the 2-week miss):

  1. Discovery — documented the as-is (manual pulls + extracts in Sheets, 1.5–2 hrs per snapshot); named the join gap (tickets without container ID); priced the status quo at >$100K/yr in the infra team's own rates including VP decision risk. Exit bar: infra prioritizes it themselves off the price; scope = the seven metrics, nothing new.
  2. Plan & Design — the BRD: seven frozen definitions, daily/hourly feed, failure alerts, a cross-reference table for the ticket join then a real key upstream; acceptance = parallel run within 1% for consecutive weeks. The RAID log — missing the data-access security review. Exit bar: BRD accepted by infra; acceptance gate published.
  3. Build & Test — extract/load specs per system, parity checks, the parallel-run procedure; when the security review surfaced mid-build I re-sequenced and owned the miss upward the same day. Exit bar: review closed (2 weeks); parallel run within 1% for consecutive weeks; mismatches explained (mostly late-arriving tickets on the manual side).
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — the cutover memo; the Monday deck moved onto the pipeline; the manual pull kept as rollback. Exit bar: first Monday off the pipeline, same seven numbers; zero WBR deliveries skipped through the build.
  5. Hypercare / Close — watched the failure alerts; triaged the first automated catch (a 4% ticket-sync drop invisible in the manual era); definitions into code; institutionalized the kickoff question. Exit bar: accuracy 60% → 90%; the pipeline steers the review without me.

The build was the infra team's — the ETL → S3 → SQS → Snowflake → Looker pipeline and the failure alerts; mine were the as-is documentation, the >$100K pricing, the BRD's seven frozen definitions, the acceptance gate, the parallel-run procedure and the cutover memo. The contribution: pricing the status quo in the owning team's own loaded rates — including VP decision risk, not just hours — so infra prioritized the build themselves (the >$100K/yr is a priced baseline, not claimed savings), and the acceptance gate: parallel run within 1% per frozen metric for consecutive weeks, every mismatch explained by cause.

The obstacles: the join gap — shipped with a documented cross-reference table while the real key was pushed upstream to Support — and the unplanned security review: two weeks lost, re-sequenced, owned upward the same day, with the WBR kept alive on the manual pull so zero Mondays were skipped. Scope held to the seven metrics, nothing new — new metrics traded for stability; buy-in came from the price in their own rates, and the parity gate made the cutover unarguable — the VP saw the same seven numbers on the first Monday off the pipeline. What I challenged was my own category error — treating the security review as an engineering concern rather than a program dependency with a lead time; the fix is institutional: every kickoff now asks whose approval the data needs and what its lead time is.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: WBR accuracy 60% → 90%; bus factor 1 → ≥2; first automated catch: a 4% ticket-sync drop. >$100K/yr priced baseline (record of the pricing).

  • $ — >$100K/yr priced baseline in the infra team's own loaded rates (record of the pricing) — priced, not claimed as savings; my model splits it ≈$30K hours + ≈$70K decision risk, both modeled.
  • # — Four systems → one Snowflake pipeline; bus factor 1 → ≥2 (record); zero WBR deliveries skipped through the build (record).
  • % — WBR accuracy 60% → 90%; parallel-run parity within 1% for consecutive weeks; first automated catch a 4% ticket-sync drop invisible in the manual era (record).
  • Time — The security-review miss cost two weeks — owned upward the same day, build re-sequenced, review closed (record).
  • Customer — The VP steered on the same seven numbers — the first Monday off the pipeline showed the same seven, and the manual pull stayed as rollback until parity had been earned and held a week beyond (record).
  • Quality — Seven frozen definitions moved into code (no drift); every parallel-run mismatch explained by cause — mostly late-arriving tickets on the manual side (record).

Against the goal stated above: WBR accuracy 60 → 90%; bus factor 1 → ≥2; the seven frozen definitions moved into code; and the first automated catch — a 4% ticket-sync drop invisible in the manual era — with the pipeline now steering the review without me. The trades: two weeks of date for a security review I should have planned, and new metrics for stability — the BRD froze the seven; the manual pull stayed as rollback until parity had been earned and held a week beyond.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Ownership?

Because both halves are ownership: taking the bus-factor-1 system I had built and writing it out of my own hands, and owning the security-review miss upward the same day it surfaced — with zero review deliveries skipped while I paid for it.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Ownership?

S1 — taking the definition nobody owned — is the backup; S14, the seam between OMS and WMS, is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Flexport — recent. The current-role Ownership record is S1: taking the “available” definition nobody owned through three signatures.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Accuracy 60 → 90% and bus factor 1 → ≥2 are the record results the build existed for; the first automated catch — a 4% ticket-sync drop invisible in the manual era — is the proof the pipeline sees what the Sheets could not.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the >$100K/yr is a priced baseline (the cost of the manual status quo in infra's own rates, including VP decision risk), not a claimed saving; my $30K/$70K split of it is modeled, and I will not call any of it realized savings.

Probing — I'm concerned about the decision-risk dollar — tell me more.

Yes — it is the soft part: one wrong number in ten becoming a wrong steer worth a squad-week, range $12K to $117K. I'd firm it up by tagging every WBR action item for a quarter and counting how many were reversed when the number was corrected.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Security, privacy and data-access reviews are program dependencies with lead times — I paid for that lesson once. The standing question at every kickoff: whose approval does this data need, and what is its lead time?

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

The Ownership pattern — price the status quo in the owning team's own rates so they prioritize it themselves, keep the old path alive until the new one has earned parity, and treat security and data-access reviews as dependencies with lead times.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The BRD's acceptance gate was parity within 1% for consecutive weeks — met, with most mismatches traced to the manual side; accuracy went 60 → 90%, the bus factor above one, and the pipeline steers the review without me.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (12). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the as-is was documented — four systems, Sheets, 1.5–2 hours per snapshot, tickets without a container ID — and the status quo was priced in the infra team's own rates. They prioritized it off that number.

Who wrote the requirements?

I did — nobody had written requirements for a program's operating metrics. The BRD carried the seven frozen definitions and the acceptance gate.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at BRD acceptance — and what wasn't?

On it: the join gap, parity failure, bus factor one. Not on it: the data-access security review. That omission cost two weeks and is the item I now add at every kickoff.

How did you handle the join problem in design?

A documented cross-reference table so the pipeline could ship, then a real key pushed upstream to Support. Ship with the bridge, fix the source.

Build & Test — What was your acceptance test?

Manual versus pipeline within 1% for consecutive weeks, every mismatch explained by cause. Most mismatches turned out to be the manual side — late-arriving tickets.

Parity within 1% for consecutive weeks and the review closed. The deck source switched; the VP saw the same seven numbers.

What was your rollback?

The manual pull — kept running until the pipeline had earned consecutive weeks of parity, and a week beyond.

Hypercare — How did you know it was working?

The first automated catch — a 4% ticket-sync drop invisible in the manual era — and accuracy moving from 60% to 90%. The alerts came to me, not silence.

What was your definition of "stable" after cutover?

Failure alerts arriving to me, not silence; the seven numbers matching the retired manual pull for a week beyond the parity gate; and the first real catch triaged to a source cause. Then the manual pull was retired.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

New metrics for stability, and two weeks of date for a security review I should have planned. I would trade the second again; I would plan it now. Escalated the two-week miss, to my leadership, the day it surfaced.

Why didn't you know about the review?

I treated it as an engineering concern rather than a program dependency with a lead time. That's the category error I fixed — the standing question now: whose approval does this data need, and what is its lead time?

Where this lands at Amazon: security, privacy and data-access reviews are program dependencies with lead times — I paid for that lesson once and now ask the question at every kickoff. The rest is the Ownership pattern: price the status quo in the owning team's own rates so they prioritize it themselves, and keep the old path alive until the new one has earned parity.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The record number is a price, not a saving: the manual status quo was priced at more than $100K a year in the infra team's own loaded rates, and that number is what got it prioritized. My model shows what sits inside it: about $30K is my hours, roughly four a week, and about $70K is decision risk, a VP steering on a number that was wrong forty percent of the time. Taking accuracy from sixty to ninety retires about $50K of that risk, and getting the bus factor above one is worth maybe $15K a year in expected loss. So the >$100K is record and priced; the $30K, $50K and $15K splits are modeled, and I will not call any of it realized savings."

If challenged — the full answers.

The decision-risk number is the soft part.

Yes, it rests on one wrong number in ten becoming a wrong steer worth a squad- week; the range runs from $12K to $117K. I'd firm it up by tagging every WBR action item for a quarter and counting how many were reversed when the number was corrected.

You never claimed the $100K, so why is it here?

Because it's the number the infra team used to prioritize the build; it's a priced baseline in their rates. What I'd measure now is the hours actually retired and whether any Monday number was restated after cutover, which is the honest realized version.

Bus factor is not a dollar.

Agreed, so I priced it as expected loss: twenty percent attrition times the rebuild plus blind weeks. The real test is whether the seven numbers publish when I'm on vacation, and after cutover they did.

S8 — Elementum: replacing brochure lead times with measured lane distributions — promise at p99, safety stock released by real variability

Amazon LPs: Dive Deep (primary) · Are Right, A Lot (backup — measured vs brochure) · Frugality (the release). Crosswalk: loop packet S3 (+S4's math) — consistent. A different program from S6's $2M — never add them.

Honesty label. I owned the requirement (measured lanes, not brochures), the p99 promise policy and the release rule; the data team built the distributions; planners sized the buffers; Finance signed the release. The safety-stock formula is how I explain it now with the MIT inventory-theory toolkit — an illustration, not a program artifact.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Planning ran on carrier-brochure lead times — buffers sized on a variability nobody had measured. Task. I owned the requirement, the promise policy, and the release rule; the data team built the distributions; planners sized the buffers. Action. Discovery: the ask was a 'variability report'; I reframed it as a promise-and-buffer policy. Plan & Design: promise at p99 per lane — the smallest d with P(L≤d) ≥ 0.99 — not average-plus-padding; release rule as a signed planning act with Finance. Build & Test: actual transit and production cycle measured on 200 lanes; drift flag and refresh cadence. Cutover: I would not endorse the cut until lead-time accuracy hit 99% on that set. Hypercare: post-release stockout-and-expedite watch; fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves. Lesson: fat tails keep their buffer; I owned the rule, not the model as a day job. Result. Primary metric: lead-time accuracy on 200 lanes — brochure lead times → 99% at the p99 promise. $ impact: 580K units / ~$1.5M of safety stock released without a stockout (record, one-time working capital), with ≈$0.6M/yr modeled around it — a different program from S6's $2M; never add them."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The six-site manufacturer's logistics manager asked for a "lead-time variability report." The real problem: inventory planning ran on carrier-brochure lead times — every buffer sized on a variability nobody had measured; too much buffer on reliable lanes, too little on fat-tailed ones.

T — Nobody owned the seam between transportation reality and planning parameters; I took it.

The goal: measured lanes replace catalog values, a promise at a defensible tail, and buffer released only on evidence — scoped at 200 lanes by site, lane and mode. The risk cut both ways: keep planning on brochures and you hold too much on reliable lanes and stock out on fat-tailed ones; cut buffer without evidence and the stockout is yours — and an unmaintained promise decays into a new brochure, which is why every parameter got a drift flag.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: lead-time parameters — carrier-brochure values, variability unmeasured; target measured distributions with lead-time accuracy gated at 99% on the 200-lane set. Buffer capital — sized on catalog values; target released only under a signed evidence rule, with service held.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (dependency: the six-site canonical model (S7) — six definitions cannot produce one σ; lane-cohort counts and week markers are not in the record):

  1. Discovery — reframed the report request as a parameter problem; scoped 200 lanes by site/lane/mode with success criteria (measured lanes replace catalog values; buffer released only on evidence); showed one right-skewed lane where the average hid the tail. Exit bar: planning and logistics agree the problem is the parameter, not the report.
  2. Plan & Design — wrote the requirement to the data team (mean, spread, tail per lane; production cycle + inbound transit vs actual), the p99 promise policy, the release rule as a signed planning act (fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves — refused as a blanket release, agreed as a rule), the stockout-and-expedite watch, the drift flag and refresh cadence. On p95 vs p99 I showed the asymmetry per lane and left the percentile choice to planning — their choice, my frame. Exit bar: policy signed by planning; Finance agrees the release rule in the room; data team accepts the spec.
  3. Build & Test — data team built the lane distributions and tracked lead-time accuracy per lane; I showed the asymmetry per lane (line-down cost vs carrying cost). Exit bar: lead-time accuracy 99% on the 200-lane set — the bar I would not endorse a cut below.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live — planners executed the buffer release under the signed rule, fat-tail lanes excluded until their source process moved, the watch armed first. Exit bar: 580K units / ~$1.5M released; no stockout in the watch window.
  5. Hypercare / Close — read the stockout-and-expedite watch, set drift flags per lane, handed the refresh cadence to planning. Exit bar: watch clean; the promise stays measured rather than decaying into a new brochure.

I owned the requirement (measured lanes, not brochures), the p99 promise policy and the release rule; the data team built the distributions and tracked accuracy; planners sized the buffers and executed the release; Finance signed each lane. The contribution was reframing a report request into a promise-and-buffer policy, and writing the release rule as a signed planning act with the exception inside it — fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves — so the rule did the refusing when Finance pushed for a broader release.

The obstacle was that push — answered with the rule Finance had signed and the lanes' asymmetry; the percentile fight never happened, because I showed each lane's line-down cost against carrying cost and left the choice to planning, and they chose p99. Scope was 200 lanes by site, lane and mode with success criteria written at discovery; the release went lane by lane under the signed rule with the stockout-and-expedite watch armed first, and planning signed the policy with Finance agreeing the release rule in the same room, before any cut. I challenged the brochure itself — showed one right-skewed lane where the average hid the tail — and refused both averaging away a tail and endorsing a cut below 99% accuracy; both refusals were written into the act before anyone asked.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: lead-time accuracy → 99% on 200 lanes at the p99 promise. $ impact: 580K units / ~$1.5M released without a stockout (record, one-time); ≈$0.6M/yr modeled recurring around it.

  • $ — 580K units / ~$1.5M of safety stock released without a stockout (record, one-time working capital); ≈$0.6M/yr modeled recurring around it (carry at a benchmark 20% all-in rate + expedites avoided) — a different program from S6's $2M; never add them.
  • # — 200 lanes measured by site, lane and mode; 580K units released (record).
  • % — Lead-time accuracy 99% on the 200-lane set at the p99 promise — the bar I would not endorse a cut below (record).
  • Customer — Service held: zero stockouts on released lanes through the watch window (record).
  • Quality — Release only under the signed rule — fat-tail lanes kept buffer until the source process moved; drift flags and a refresh cadence keep the promise measured, never a new brochure (record).

Against the goal stated above: measured lanes replaced catalog values at 99% lead-time accuracy on the 200-lane set; 580K units / ~$1.5M of safety stock released without a stockout; fat tails kept their buffer — all three discovery criteria held. The trade: capital stayed tied up where the tail said it must, and release speed was traded for a release with zero stockouts in the watch window.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Dive Deep?

Because the whole story is refusing the surface request — a report — and measuring the underlying distributions: mean, spread and tail per lane, production cycle plus inbound transit vs actual, with p99 defined cumulatively because a right-skewed lane's normal shortcut understates the tail.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Dive Deep?

S10 — Austria, where I measured the measurement — is the backup; S3 (the grain problem) and S7 (the phantom math) are thirds.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. The current-role story with the same LP is S3 — aggregates de-aggregated to the cent — and S10 is the same era's backup.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

The release — 580K units / ~$1.5M without a stockout — is the record that proves the promise, and 99% lead-time accuracy on 200 lanes is the bar that made it safe. Service held is the result planners feel; a release that causes a stockout is worse than no release.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY — the ~$1.5M is a one-time working-capital release, booked once as a signed planning act, not an annual saving; what recurs is my model (≈$0.3M carry at a benchmark rate plus avoided expedites), and I never add this program to S6's $2M.

Probing — I'm concerned about the expedite number — tell me more.

The softest — it assumes ten thousand shipments a year on the two hundred lanes and one late arrival in ten forcing an expedite before p99. To firm it I would pull the expedite ledger by lane for the year before and after the release and compare directly.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Fat tails keep their buffer, and I owned the rule, not the model as a day job. And give every parameter a drift flag so the promise never decays into a new brochure.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Inbound lead-time variability is half of every buffer and every promise — measure the lane's own distribution, promise at the tail you can defend, release capital only on evidence with the exception written into the rule, and give every parameter a drift flag.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The success criteria from discovery were: measured lanes replace catalog values, buffer released only on evidence, fat tails keep buffer. All three held — 99% accuracy at the p99 promise, 580K units / ~$1.5M out, no stockout in the watch window.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When planning and logistics agreed the problem was the parameter, not the report — brochure lead times sizing every buffer — and the 200-lane scope by site, lane and mode was written. The canonical model from the six-site program made one σ possible.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote them — measured lanes replace catalog values; buffer released only on evidence; fat tails keep buffer. Planning signed the policy; Finance agreed the release rule in the same room.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

The average hiding the tail, a buffer cut before evidence, and the promise decaying into a new brochure. Mitigations: cumulative p99 per lane, a 99% accuracy bar before endorsement, drift flags with a refresh cadence.

How did you handle Finance pushing for a broader release?

With the rule they had signed — fat-tail lanes keep buffer until the source process moves. I showed the lanes and the asymmetry; the rule did the refusing.

Why p99 and not p95?

Per lane, the cost of a line-down on a fat-tail lane dwarfed the carrying cost of the extra day. I showed the asymmetry and let planning choose; they chose p99.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a lane?

Distribution built from actuals — mean, spread, tail — accuracy tracked, p99 published, drift flag set. Below 99% accuracy on the set, I did not endorse a release.

Who built the distributions?

The data team, to my requirement. I owned the requirement, the promise policy and the release rule; planners sized the buffers; Finance signed. The formula is how I explain it now with the MIT toolkit — not a program artifact.

Cutover — What made you willing to endorse the release?

Lead-time accuracy at 99% on the 200-lane set and the watch armed. Then planners released under the rule, lane by lane, with fat tails excluded.

What was your rollback trigger?

Any stockout or expedite on a released lane during the watch — buffer restored. It never triggered.

Hypercare — How did you know it was closed?

The watch stayed clean, 580K units and about $1.5M were out without a stockout, and planning owned the refresh cadence and drift flags. The promise stayed measured.

What did you hand to planning at close?

The drift flag per lane with its refresh cadence, the p99 promise per lane, and the release rule as a signed act they could re-apply. The stockout-and-expedite watch became a standing report.

Cross-phase — Which lanes did you hold and how did the business take it?

The fat-tail lanes — buffer kept until the source process moved. Finance accepted because the exception was in the rule they signed, not a judgment I made afterwards.

What did you refuse?

Averaging away a tail, and endorsing a cut below 99% accuracy. Both were written into the act before anyone asked.

Where this lands at Amazon: inbound lead-time variability is half of every buffer and every promise. The transferable discipline: measure the lane's own distribution, promise at the tail you can defend, release capital only on evidence with the exception written into the rule, and give every parameter a drift flag so the promise never decays into a new brochure.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured result is five hundred eighty thousand units, about a million and a half dollars of safety stock, released at ninety-nine percent lead-time accuracy with zero stockouts on the watch; that is record and Finance signed it. What recurs around it I model at roughly six hundred thousand a year: about three hundred thousand in carrying cost on capital we no longer hold, and about three hundred thousand in expedites we no longer pay because the promise sits at the ninety-ninth percentile instead of a brochure number. The release and the accuracy are measured; the two recurring pieces are my model on benchmark carrying and expedite rates, and I never add this to the two million from the forecast program."

If challenged — the full answers.

The expedite number is the softest

It assumes ten thousand shipments a year on the two hundred lanes and that one late arrival in ten forced an expedite before p99; to firm it I would pull the expedite ledger by lane for the year before and after the release and compare directly.

Twenty percent carrying cost is high

Fifteen to twenty-five is the textbook range and I use the middle; capital alone is eight to twelve, so if Finance wants capital only the carry is about a hundred and fifty thousand and I would say that instead.

Why is the million and a half not an annual saving

Because it is a one-time working-capital release, booked once as a signed planning act; what recurs is the carry and the avoided expedites, and I would rather be right at six hundred thousand a year than wrong at a million and a half.

S6 — Elementum: eight SAP data domains into one real-time supply/demand view — a shortage visible a lead-time early

Amazon LPs: Deliver Results (third) · Earn Trust (the two-signal release rule) · Dive Deep · Think Big (one view over eight domains). Crosswalk: loop packet S5 — consistent (70→85%, $2M, both-errors rule). Different program from S7 (the six-site ingest) and S8 (the $1.5M lanes) — never merge the dollars or the timelines.

Honesty label. I owned the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery; the client's SAP teams and our integration engineers built the feeds; planners owned the forecast — I owned its measurement and the release rule. I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it.
60-second pitch

"Situation. A six-site manufacturer learned about component shortages when the line stopped; eight SAP domains never met, so 'which orders are at risk' had no data path. Task. I took the definitions, the data-integrity program, and the delivery. Action. Discovery: profiled all eight domains; signed gap findings. Plan & Design: supply = on-hand + production plan + released planned orders only; grain SKU × location × date; shortage alert = days-of-cover vs lane lead-time p90. Build & Test: inventory and production first — numbers planners already believed; daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52. Cutover: shortage-alert product live in 3 months behind data-quality gates; nobody mandated off a spreadsheet. Hypercare: safety-stock release only where forecast error AND lead-time error both improved, signed with planning and Finance. Lesson: define supply honestly before any screen. Result. Primary metric: forecast accuracy on scoped class-A SKUs — 70% → 85%. $ impact: $2M of safety stock released under the both-errors-improved rule (record, one-time working capital), with ≈$0.8M/yr modeled recurring value around it — separate from the $1.5M lane program; never add them."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — A strategic manufacturing customer with six sites (mix of make-to-order and make-to-stock): planners learned about a component shortage when the line stopped; S&OP ran off private spreadsheets; shipment data was 60% complete; supplier, material, BOM, inventory, production, transportation, sales-order and planning data all lived in SAP and site systems but never met. Leadership's question — "which customer orders are at risk this quarter?" — had no data path.

T — Nobody owned the seam; there was no spec, no agreed grain, no definition of "supply," no data-quality bar. I chose all four, as owner of the definitions, the data-integrity program and the delivery.

The goal: a real-time supply/demand view with honest definitions and a shortage visible a lead-time early. Beyond the data itself, the challenge was planner trust — and left alone, the line-down surprises continue, with expedites instead of re-plans and buffers sized on noise.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: forecast accuracy — 70% on scoped class-A SKUs; shortage detection — at line-down, target a lead-time early; shipment-data completeness — 60% at the start; planner effort on reconciliation and firefighting — the baseline the program had to cut.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (canonical plan: Discovery 4–6 wks → master-data foundation 4–6 wks parallel → domain waves 8–12 wks → views live 4 wks → scale & hypercare 4 wks; the 3-month figure is the shortage-alert product; the six-site ingest is S7's separate 5-month program):

  1. Discovery (4–6 wks) — profiled every SAP domain (completeness, latency, joinability) to my own gap templates; wrote the definitions memo (grain SKU × location × date; supply = on-hand + production plan + released planned orders only) and the metrics tree. When Planning asked to count unfirmed MRP orders as supply because it looked better, I refused: unfirmed orders breathe every run. Exit bar: signed gap findings per domain; scope frozen in writing; planners agree the supply definition before any screen.
  2. Plan & Design (parallel) — master-data foundation plan; MPN mapping worklist with its coverage gate; BOM extract and phantom clean-up plan; domain-wave sequence by trust; DQ scorecard spec; the 10-step onboarding playbook. Exit bar: master-data gates green; unmapped-MPN list under threshold with a named owner; wave order signed by domain owners.
  3. Build & Test (8–12 wks) — domain waves behind published DQ gates; shortage-alert rule specified (days-of-cover vs lane LT p90); daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52; weekly root-cause loop with false alarms worked as defects. Exit bar: per-domain completeness ≥ gate; reconciliation passing daily; false alarms trending down; pilot planners using the alert.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (4 wks) — views live for pilot planners with the at-risk list replacing the Monday spreadsheet; go/no-go read from the scorecards. Exit bar: planners answer "where is my product" from the platform in the live S&OP meeting — not from a spreadsheet.
  5. Hypercare / Close (4 wks) — exception workbench staffed; weekly root-cause reviews; the safety-stock release rule signed with Planning and Finance. Exit bar: blockers/day under threshold two consecutive weeks; release applied only where forecast AND lead-time error both improved.

The client's SAP teams and our integration engineers built the feeds; planners owned the forecast — I owned its measurement and the release rule, and I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it. Mine specifically: the definitions memo, the metrics tree, the DQ scorecard spec, the wave sequence, the release rule and the 10-step onboarding playbook; domain owners held sign-off authority over their own gates. The contributions that mattered most: honest definitions — refusing to count unfirmed MRP orders as supply because it looked better — and the two-signal release rule, buffer out only where forecast error and lead-time error both improved, which is why the release survived audit.

The obstacles were planner trust and the MPN mapping worklist, which gated longer than planned. Trust was built by sequencing waves by trust — inventory and production first, numbers planners already believed — and by working false alarms as defects; the MPN slip went to the sponsor as a re-sequenced date with the coverage number attached. Priorities: waves by trust, class-A SKUs before the tail, every feed behind a published DQ gate — below the gate, the feed got fixed, not automated. Buy-in: domain owners could fail their own wave — the business could say no to its own data — and nobody was mandated off the spreadsheet. The decisions challenged: Planning asked to count unfirmed MRP orders as supply because it looked better — refused, since unfirmed orders change every run, amplify noise and inflate supply; and the safety-stock release was refused as a blanket act and agreed as a rule.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: forecast accuracy 70% → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs (record). $2M safety stock released under the both-errors rule (record, one-time); planner effort −50%; shortage alert live in 3 months; adoption 8×.

  • $ — $2M safety stock released (record, one-time working capital, under the both-errors-improved rule signed with Planning and Finance) + ≈$0.8M/yr modeled recurring (carry, expedites pre-empted, planner capacity) — never added to S8's $1.5M.
  • # — Eight SAP data domains into one view across six sites; shortage-alert product live in 3 months; adoption 8× (record).
  • % — Forecast accuracy 70% → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs (record); planner effort −50% (record); shipment data 60% complete at the start.
  • Time — Shortage alert fires at days-of-cover ≤ lane lead-time p90 — a lead-time early instead of at line-down (record mechanism); views live in 3 months.
  • Customer — “Which customer orders are at risk this quarter?” answered from the platform in the live S&OP meeting, not a spreadsheet (record exit bar).
  • Quality — Daily reconciliation to MD04/COOIS/VA05/MB52; false alarms classified weekly to root cause and burned down as defects (record).

Against the goal stated above: forecast accuracy 70 → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs; the shortage alert live in three months, firing a lead-time early instead of at line-down; planner effort −50%; the $2M safety-stock release under the both-errors-improved rule Planning and Finance signed. The trades: the lane tail and the MPN tail were deferred behind the same gates — priced in coverage, not count — rollback stayed free because the spreadsheet was never banned, and the release rule traded speed of release for a release that survived audit.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Deliver Results?

Because the results landed behind gates and signatures: forecast accuracy 70 → 85% on scoped class-A SKUs, the shortage alert live in three months, and $2M of safety stock out under the both-errors-improved rule Planning and Finance signed. Delivering the number honestly — released planned orders only — is the point.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Deliver Results?

S11 — 7 → 68 behind graduation gates — is the primary Deliver Results story; S18 (8 months, both hubs, gate cleared) is the backup, and S2's comingle launch the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

This is from the Elementum years — older. The most recent same-LP record is S2's comingle launch in my current role (zero incidents, +15% signed); S11 at Flexport sits between.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Forecast accuracy 70 → 85% is the record headline and the $2M release is the signed, audit-surviving consequence; planner effort −50% and the alert live in three months are what the planners felt. Adoption 8× mattered because trust was the real product.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY revenue figure — the 70 → 85% is forecast accuracy on scoped class-A SKUs against the pre-state, and the $2M is a one-time working-capital release, not an annual saving; the recurring value I model is ≈$0.8M/yr around it, labeled.

Probing — I'm concerned about the expedites-avoided figure — tell me more.

The softest row — about sixty expedites a year pre-empted at a five-thousand-dollar premium, both illustrative. To firm it I would pull the expedited-freight ledger for the six sites for the twelve months before and after the alert went live and count the premium directly.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Define supply honestly before any screen. Differently: start the MPN-mapping worklist on day one — it is never “done” and it gated more than I planned.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Supply/demand visibility built honestly — define supply before the screen, measure MAPE and bias at the decision grain, alert a lead-time early, and release buffer only when both error signals improve. The two-signal release rule is the part most rooms have never seen.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

Leadership's question was “which customer orders are at risk this quarter?” — answered a lead-time early from the platform in the live S&OP meeting, with accuracy 70 → 85%, the alert live in three months, and the $2M release under the signed rule.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (14). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

Every domain had a signed gap finding on its own profiled data and the metrics tree was agreed. Four to six weeks; scope frozen in writing at the exit.

Who wrote the success criteria and who signed?

I wrote the metrics tree — OTIF, inventory dollars, MAPE/bias, lane lead-time accuracy, completeness, latency, adoption — and the domain owners with sign-off authority signed their gates.

What did you define before any screen?

Grain — SKU × location × date — and supply: on-hand plus production plan plus released planned orders only. Planners accepted the definition first; the view was built to it.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at sign-off?

MPN mapping never done, carrier EDI capability spread, planner trust, and SAP extract load — carried openly with a mitigation and owner each. MPN became a standing metric rather than a task.

How did you sequence the waves?

By trust: inventory and production first because planners already believed those numbers, then purchasing/ASN, then transportation with the best lanes first. Class-A SKUs before the tail.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a feed?

Its DQ scorecard — completeness, latency, accuracy vs source, joinability — above the published gate, and daily reconciliation passing. Below the gate, the feed got fixed, not automated.

How did you treat false alarms?

As defects: classified weekly to a root cause — mapping gap, master-data defect, carrier capability, pipeline bug — owned and burned down. Adoption followed planners watching their reported defects die.

Who could fail a wave?

The domain owner — they held sign-off authority over their gate. I designed it so the business could say no to its own data.

Cutover — What was the go-live gate for the views?

Planners answering "where is my product" from the platform in the live S&OP meeting rather than from a spreadsheet. The shortage alert was live in three months.

What was your rollback?

The spreadsheet — still there, never banned. Reversibility was free because I never mandated anyone off it.

Hypercare — How did you decide hypercare was over? What did it hand over?

Blockers/day under threshold two consecutive weeks, adoption targets met, the exception workbench staffed. Handover: the weekly root-cause review to domain owners, the onboarding playbook (profile → map → test file → parallel run → scorecard → live), and the MPN exception queue as a standing metric.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

The lane tail and the MPN tail — deferred behind the same gates, priced in coverage, not count. Escalated the MPN worklist gating longer than planned — to the sponsor as a re-sequenced date with the coverage number attached.

Who owned the forecast?

The planners. I owned the accuracy measurement (MAPE/bias per SKU family), input integrity and the release rule. I have not owned a demand forecast end-to-end and won't claim it.

Why released planned orders only? · Do differently?

Unfirmed MRP orders change every run; planning against them amplifies noise and inflates supply. Differently: start the MPN-mapping worklist on day one — it is never "done" and gated more than I planned.

Where this lands at Amazon: this is supply/demand visibility built honestly — define supply before the screen, measure MAPE and bias at the decision grain, alert a lead-time early, and release buffer only when both error signals improve. The two-signal release rule is the part most rooms have never seen.

The math — step-by-step derivation

Table 1 — before: the problem in numbers.

StepQuantityFormula → substitutionInputs (basis)Value
1Forecast accuracyaccuracy = 1 − MAPE = 1 − 0.30 = 70%scoped class-A SKUs (record)70%
2What the error math looks likeMAPE = (30/100 + 10/120 + 20/80 + 10/100)/4 = (0.30 + 0.083 + 0.25 + 0.10)/4 = 18.3%4 illustrative weeks: A = 100, 120, 80, 100; F = 130, 110, 100, 90; |e| = 30, 10, 20, 1018.3% on this sample; bias = (430 − 400)/400 = +7.5%
3The supply liesupply counted unfirmed MRP planned orders — they change every runrecord problemplans built on sand
4Shortage discoveryat line-down: ~150 expedite events/yrbenchmark-modeled count~150 fire-drills/yr
5Planner burn12 planners × 40% = 4.8 FTE-equivalent on reconciliationrecord share4.8 FTE reconciling, not root-causing

StepQuantityFormula → substitutionInputs (basis)Value
1Honest supplysupply(t) = on-hand + in-transit ≤ t + production ≤ t + RELEASED planned orders onlyunfirmed MRP planned orders excluded (record rule)walked below on ROUTER-100
2FG netting walk — setupFG SKU ROUTER-100 at DC-East: on-hand 400; in-transit 600 lands day 5; production 800 day 9; demand 500 d3 / 700 d7 / 400 d10illustrative (v105 §5.12)projected balance below
3Day 3PAB(3) = 400 − 500 = −100only on-hand has arrived by day 3dated shortage of 100 on day 3
4Day 7PAB(7) = (400 + 600) − (500 + 700) = 1,000 − 1,200 = −200day-9 production is too late for day-7 demandshortage of 200; fix window = 9 − 7 = 2 days
5Day 10PAB(10) = (400 + 600 + 800) − (500 + 700 + 400) = 1,800 − 1,600 = +200all supply lands by day 10+200 — the shortage is timing, not volume
6Shortage alert ruleDoC = 1,200 / 100 = 12 days; 12 ≤ p90 14 → alert nowFG SKU: on-hand 1,200 at 100/day; lane p90 = 14 days (illustrative)alert a lead-time early, not at line-down
7Counter-casep90 = 9 days: 12 > 9 → no alertsame SKU, faster lane (illustrative)3-day slack — no false alarm
8Two-signal release ruleSS = z × d × σ_L = 2.33 × 100 × 4 = 932; with σ_L 4 → 2: 2.33 × 100 × 2 = 466; release 932 − 466 = 466 unitsrelease only if forecast error AND lead-time accuracy both improved; d = 100/day, z = 2.33 at 99% (illustrative, toolkit form)466 units off this SKU
9Accuracy aftererror 30% → 15% (halved); accuracy = 1 − 0.15 = 85%gated waves; false alarms worked as defects (record)85%
