Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured number is two million dollars of safety stock released, and that is record, signed with planning and Finance under the both-errors-improved rule. Around it I model roughly eight hundred thousand a year of recurring value: about two hundred thousand in carry cost on the capital that came out, about three hundred thousand in expedite premium the shortage alert pre-empts because it fires a lead-time early instead of at line-down, and about three hundred thousand of planner capacity from the fifty percent effort cut. The two million and the fifty percent are measured; the three recurring pieces are my model on benchmark rates, and I keep them separate from the one-and-a-half million in the lane program."

If challenged — the full answers.

The expedite figure is the softest

It rests on about sixty expedites a year pre-empted at a five thousand dollar premium, both illustrative; to firm it I would pull the expedited-freight ledger for the six sites for the twelve months before and after the alert went live and count the premium directly.

Planner capacity is not cash

Agreed, it is two point four FTE of time redeployed, not headcount removed; the fifty percent is measured, the forty percent share of the week is my assumption, and a two-week time study on the S&OP prep cycle would replace it.

Why not claim the whole two million every year

Because a release is a one-time working-capital event; the recurring value is the carry at eight to twelve percent, and I would rather say two hundred thousand a year honestly than two million a year wrongly.

S9 — Elementum: 1,000 supplier/carrier routes asked, 597 built first — X12 EDI onboarding behind a ≥95% ASN cohort gate

Amazon LPs: Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit (primary — 597 vs 1,000, two levels up) · Deliver Results · Insist on the Highest Standards (the gate held twice) · Think Big (the staged 1,000-lane vision) · Broad Responsibility (the portal floor kept small carriers inside the standard). Crosswalk: loop packet S7 — consistent. Cohort sizes and the number of cohorts are not in the record — do not quote them.

Honesty label. I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor; engineers built the maps and harness to the standard; Transportation owned the carrier relationships.
60-second pitch

"Situation. The Global Head of Supply Chain wanted inbound visibility on 1,000 lanes in two months; shipment-data completeness was 60% and high-value lanes were blind. Task. I owned the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor. Action. Discovery: spend Pareto — 597 lanes = 80% of freight spend — pre-validated with Transportation and Procurement. Plan & Design: X12 850/855/856/940/945/214/997 with an XML/portal floor; cohort gate ≥95% valid ASN. Build & Test: mapping-and-test harness; scorecards per lane. Cutover: 597 live in month one; one cohort failed twice on a timestamp format — I held it. Hypercare: completeness scorecards to 95%; ~200 next wave behind the same gate. Lesson: automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — the gate is the product. Result. Primary metric: inbound shipment-data completeness — 60% → 95%. $ impact: freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record); my model splits it into routing-guide compliance, mode discipline and expedites avoided, and adds ≈$0.4M/yr of modeled document-handling labor on top; 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one; adoption 8×."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The customer's Global Head of Supply Chain wanted end-to-end inbound visibility on 1,000 global lanes in two months — scope, schedule and quality all fixed. Lanes differed by orders of magnitude in spend, volume and criticality; carriers differed in EDI capability; completeness was 60% and high-value lanes were effectively blind.

T — Own the scope analysis and the onboarding standard, influencing an executive two levels above my sponsor.

The goal: visibility that was actually trustworthy, at the spend that mattered first. The risk of taking the ask literally: automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — 1,000 lanes at 60% completeness in two months would have shipped exactly that — and without a floor for carriers without EDI, the long tail gets priced out of the standard.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: shipment-data completeness — 60%; high-value lane visibility — effectively blind, target 100% on the lanes carrying the spend; the activation standard — none existed, target a ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate a lane had to clear before going live.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery — built the spend Pareto and lane scoring model (spend × criticality × mode × volume) and the carrier EDI-capability survey; pre-validated the 597 / ~200 / remainder scope with Transportation and Procurement before taking it up. Exit bar: lane owners cannot contradict the cut; the executive accepts the phased plan in spend share, not lane count.
  2. Plan & Design — wrote the onboarding standard (document set + XML/portal floor — "the standard was the data, not the transport"), the ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate, the per-lane scorecard spec (events received / expected; 997 rate; tracking-number joinability), the harness design. Exit bar: standard signed by Transportation; gate published to carriers before cohort 1; harness templates exist.
  3. Build & Test — held engineers to the harness (per-partner mapping templates, test scripts, DQ checklist), test files per carrier, a parallel run per lane, scorecards live. Exit bar: each cohort's lanes clear ≥95% valid ASN in test before activation.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (per cohort) — activated cohorts only off the published gate; published the scorecards; recorded the hold decision when a cohort failed. Exit bar: cohort active only after the gate; a failing cohort held — twice, on a timestamp format.
  5. Hypercare / Close — weekly completeness scorecards; harness reused for later waves; method written up as the sourcing SOP (spend file in → phased plan out). Exit bar: completeness 60% → 95%; 100% visibility on high-value lanes; remaining waves behind the same gate.

The scope analysis and the standard were mine end to end — the spend Pareto and lane scoring model, the carrier EDI-capability survey, the onboarding standard, the gate, the scorecard spec and the hold decisions; engineers built the mapping templates, test scripts and harness, and Transportation held the carrier relationships and accepted the holds. The contribution: pre-validating the 597 cut with Transportation and Procurement so no lane owner could contradict it in the leadership meeting, and publishing the ≥95% gate to carriers before cohort one — a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion.

The obstacles: carriers unable to produce valid ASNs — answered with the XML/portal floor (“the standard was the data, not the transport”) and a hold rule; a cohort that failed the gate twice on a timestamp format — held both times, fixed, then activated; bespoke maps per partner — avoided by the parameterized harness. Priorities ran on spend share, not lane count — 597 lanes carrying 80% of spend first, ~200 next, the remainder after, each behind the same gate; buy-in came from the pre-validation, then the executive accepted the phased plan off the curve. The decision challenged was the 1,000-in-two-months ask itself — taken two levels up with the spend curve, deferring ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend, priced — and I refused activating any cohort that failed the gate.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: completeness 60% → 95%; 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one; freight −10% ≈ $2M in six months (record); adoption 8×.

  • $ — Freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months on the onboarded lanes (record); my three-way split (routing-guide compliance, mode discipline, expedites and detention) decomposes the record, never adds to it; ≈$0.4M/yr modeled document-handling labor on top.
  • # — 597 of 1,000 lanes = 80% of freight spend live in month one; ~200 the next wave; the remainder behind the same gate (record).
  • % — Completeness 60% → 95%; the ≥95% valid-ASN cohort gate — held twice on a cohort that failed on a timestamp format (record).
  • Time — 597 lanes live in month one; the record $2M landed within six months (record).
  • Customer — 100% visibility on high-value lanes; small carriers kept inside the standard by the XML/portal floor instead of being priced out (record).
  • Quality — The gate is the product — automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers; adoption 8×; the method written up as the sourcing SOP (record).

Against the goal stated above: completeness 60 → 95%; 100% visibility on high-value lanes; 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one at ≥95% valid ASN, the remaining waves behind the same gate — with freight spend −10% ≈ $2M in six months funding the next wave. The trade was explicit and priced: ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend deferred so the 597 could go live at the highest data quality — and quality was never traded: a lane that slipped below its scorecard went back to “fix,” not “live.”

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit?

Because I took the re-scope — 597 lanes first, not 1,000 — two levels up to the Global Head of Supply Chain with the spend curve and the lane owners' validation attached, then committed to delivering the remainder behind the same gate. And the gate held in public: one cohort failed twice on a timestamp format and I held it both times.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Have Backbone; Disagree & Commit?

S14 — refusing the quiet fix — is the backup; S5 (the council without owning a system) and S2 (the throttle) are the thirds.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. For current-role records of the same LP, S5 and S2's throttle are the ones I would pair with this.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Completeness 60 → 95% is the record the whole program was for, and freight −10% ≈ $2M in six months is what the executive funded the next wave on. The 597 = 80% of spend live in month one is the proof the re-scope was right.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the $2M is the record as measured: minus ten percent on the onboarded lanes' spend in six months, kept as stated rather than doubled into a run-rate. My three-way split of it decomposes the record, never adds to it.

Probing — I'm concerned about your split of the $2M — tell me more.

The split is a judgment on how the ten points divide; to firm it I would tag every invoice line on the onboarded lanes by cause — off-guide carrier, mode change, expedite — for the six months and let the ledger set the shares.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers — the gate is the product. Differently: the harness from cohort one, not cohort two.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Vendor and carrier onboarding at scale is a Pareto plus a gate — spend share first, a published data-quality bar a partner must clear before activation, and a floor (portal/templates) so the long tail is kept inside the standard rather than priced out of it.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The ask was 1,000 lanes in two months; the delivery was 597 lanes = 80% of spend live in month one at ≥95% valid ASN, completeness 60 → 95%, the remaining waves behind the same gate — and the executive accepted the phased plan in spend share, not lane count.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the spend Pareto was pre-validated by Transportation and Procurement and the scope proposal — 597 first, ~200 next, remainder after — could not be contradicted by any lane owner in the leadership meeting.

Who signed the scope?

The Global Head of Supply Chain accepted it after seeing the curve; the lane owners had already validated it. I priced the deferral in spend share, not lane count.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Carriers unable to produce valid ASNs, the scope being contradicted in the room, and bespoke maps per partner. Mitigations: XML/portal floor plus a hold rule, pre-validation, and a parameterized harness.

What was the entry bar for a cohort?

Standard and gate published to its carriers, mapping templates instantiated, test files in hand, a scorecard per lane. Then ≥95% valid ASN in test before activation.

How did you set the standard?

850/855/856/940/945/214/997 on X12, with XML or portal templates as the floor for carriers without EDI. The standard was the data, not the transport.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a lane?

Valid-ASN rate ≥95%, 997 acknowledgments flowing, tracking numbers joinable, completeness on its scorecard. Below that, the lane was fixed, not automated.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The cohort test day: every carrier's file through the harness in one sitting; defects classified as format, timestamp, missing segment or join key, each with an owner. That is where the timestamp defect surfaced.

Cutover — Which cohort did you hold and how did the business take it?

One cohort failed twice on a carrier timestamp format; I held it both times. Transportation accepted because the 95% gate was published before cohort one — a gate that only holds in private is a suggestion.

What did each wave deliver?

Wave 1: 597 lanes, 80% of spend, live in month one. Wave 2: ~200 lanes on a harness that made the work mostly configuration. Wave 3: the tail, including non-EDI carriers on templates.

Hypercare — How did you know you were done? What did you watch?

Completeness 60% → 95%, 100% visibility on high-value lanes, the harness reused without me, the method adopted as the sourcing SOP. Watched weekly: per-lane completeness, the 997 rate, tracking-number joinability. A lane that slipped below its scorecard went back to "fix," not "live."

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you escalate?

Deferred ~400 lanes carrying ~20% of spend so the 597 could go live at the highest data quality in month one — priced, and delivered later behind the same gate. Escalated the re-scope to the Global Head of Supply Chain — two levels up — with the spend curve and the lane owners' validation attached. Refused: activating a cohort that failed the gate.

Did you deliver the remaining 400? · Do differently?

Yes — later waves, same gate; the first 597 carried the spend and the decision. Differently: the harness from cohort one, not cohort two.

Where this lands at Amazon: vendor and carrier onboarding at scale is a Pareto plus a gate: spend share first, a published data-quality bar a partner must clear before activation, and a floor (portal/templates) so the long tail is kept inside the standard rather than priced out of it. The sentence that travels: automating dirty data manufactures confident wrong answers.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured number is freight spend down ten percent, about two million dollars in six months on the five hundred ninety-seven lanes that carried eighty percent of spend, with completeness sixty to ninety-five percent and adoption eight x. Inside that two million I model roughly eight hundred thousand from catching off-guide carrier spend, six hundred thousand from mode discipline once the ETA was trusted, and six hundred thousand from expedites and detention that chase calls used to cause; those three are my split, the two million is the record. On top of it I model about four hundred thousand a year of document-handling labor that X12 replaced, and that piece is entirely mine."

If challenged — the full answers.

The split of the two million is the softest

It is a judgment on how the ten points divide; to firm it I would tag every invoice line on the onboarded lanes by cause, off-guide carrier, mode change, or expedite, for the six months and let the ledger set the shares.

Ninety thousand documents is a guess

Fifty shipments a lane and three touched documents each is illustrative; the ISA and GS control-number counts on the VAN for the first full quarter would give the exact document volume in an afternoon.

Labor saved is not cash

Correct, nine thousand hours is coordinator capacity moved from keying to exceptions; I would measure it as headcount held flat while lanes went from six hundred to a thousand, which is the number Finance can see.

S7 — Elementum: making a contract manufacturer's BOM safe to plan on — phantoms, revision overlaps, one supply equation

Amazon LPs: Learn and Be Curious (primary — learned CM data by math) · Dive Deep · Earn Trust (external partner). Crosswalk: loop packet S6 — consistent. Guardrails: "six sites," never "6 NA sites"; no "500+ product families"; 60→95% belongs to S9; no tolerance value — the record doesn't carry one.

Honesty label. I owned the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan and the weekly Flextronics call; engineers built the normalization layer and quarantine to the spec; the commercial relationship belonged to the customer's procurement — my leverage was evidence. The $1.5M/580K belongs to the p99 program this backbone enabled (S8).
60-second pitch

"Situation. Flextronics supplied the BOM our component-demand visibility depended on; phantoms double-time demand if ingested as stocked, and overlapping revisions rewrite history if you auto-load one. Task. I owned ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan, and the weekly Flextronics call. Action. Discovery: sampled finished goods, exploded BOMs, compared component counts to Flextronics MRP — phantoms found by math. Plan & Design: phantom flatten with an explicit marker; effective-date quarantine, human disposition, never auto-load; supply equation signed. Build & Test: mapping tables owned by named site super-users; per-site reconciliation is the go-live gate. Cutover: three waves / six sites / 5 months; wave 3's hostile ERP used snapshot-diffing — 6 weeks vs a 3-week average, priced up front. Hypercare: the weekly call became standing, framed as their protection. Lesson: reconcile to the source's math, not its documentation. Result. Primary metric: contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade BOM/supply visibility — 0 → 6 in 3 waves / 5 months, zero auto-loaded revision overlaps. $ impact: ≈$0.3M/yr modeled — the six sites and zero overlaps are measured; the dollars are my model; the $1.5M belongs to S8."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Same manufacturer; Flextronics supplied the BOM and planner data that component-demand visibility depended on. Two traps documentation never confesses: phantom assemblies (logical groupings that pass demand through without stocking — ingest them as stocked and component need-dates get double-timed) and revision overlaps (two BOM revisions partially true for weeks — auto-load one and you rewrite demand history). Six site systems carried six definitions of yield, lead time and order status; MTO and MTS had different ATP semantics.

T — Own the ingest requirements, the reconciliation gate, the wave plan — and the chair of the weekly Flextronics call.

The goal: contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade BOM and supply visibility, with zero auto-loaded overlaps. The challenges beyond the traps themselves: one hostile legacy ERP with flat files and no change-data-capture, and no commercial leverage — evidence was my only lever. The consequence of ingesting naively was operational: false shortages and expedites acted on — both traps named, with evidence, in the discovery memo.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade BOM — zero; target six. Auto-loaded revision overlaps — the standing trap; target zero, ever. Manual BOM reconciliation and expedites fired at phantom shortages — the baseline the ingest layer existed to remove.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases (canonical: wave 1 = two cleanest high-volume sites; wave 2 = three sites on parameterized maps; wave 3 = the hostile legacy ERP, flat files, no change-data-capture):

  1. Discovery — sampled finished goods and exploded them against Flextronics MRP (the phantom signature found by math); catalogued the six-site definition inventory (yield, lead time, order-status semantics); wrote the MTO/MTS tagging rule and a discovery memo naming the two traps. Exit bar: Flextronics engineering confirms the phantom signature; six definitions catalogued; wave order proposed.
  2. Plan & Design — supply equation (on-hand + production plan + released planned orders); ingest requirements (phantom flatten with marker; effective-date quarantine queue with human disposition — never auto-load); the reconciliation report as the per-site gate; the 2/3/1 wave plan; mapping-table ownership model; weekly call charter. Exit bar: requirements signed by customer planning; Flextronics staffs the weekly call; wave economics accepted.
  3. Build & Test — held engineers to the spec for normalization and quarantine; reconciliation report per site; parameterized mapping templates for wave 2; snapshot-diff design for wave 3 (consecutive full extracts, checksums, a completeness flag on every extract). Exit bar: wave-1 reconciliation within tolerance; zero auto-loaded overlaps in test; completeness flag on every extract.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (per site) — each site's go-live ran off its matched reconciliation report, mapping tables handed to a named super-user, disposition queue live. The wave-3 duration went to customer leadership up front as a priced wait. Exit bar: the reconciliation report was the gate, not a date on a slide — six sites live over three waves; hardest site 6 weeks vs the 3-week average, absorbed in plan.
  5. Hypercare / Close — weekly Flextronics DQ/disposition call left standing (it had become theirs — the forum that protected their feed's reputation); the wave-2 yield gap fixed in the mapping row, model untouched; completeness flag written as a rule in the ingest layer. Exit bar: zero auto-loaded revision overlaps; phantom double-timing eliminated at source; backbone handed to the 200-lane program (S8).

Engineers built the normalization layer and quarantine to the spec; named site super-users owned the mapping tables; the commercial relationship belonged to the customer's procurement — my leverage was evidence. Mine specifically: the sampling-and-explosion analysis, the ingest requirements, the supply equation, the 2/3/1 wave plan, the gate and the call charter. The contribution: finding the phantoms by math rather than meetings, and designing the two mechanisms — the explicit phantom marker and the effective-date quarantine — that made the traps impossible rather than policed; the reconciliation report was the per-site gate, not a date on a slide.

The obstacles: an external supplier with no obligation to care — solved by making the weekly call theirs, since it protected Flextronics from blame for misses their feed didn't cause — and the hostile wave-3 ERP, handled by snapshot-diffing at six weeks against a three-week average, priced up front so leadership took it as the plan, not a slip. Priorities ran waves by cleanliness — the two cleanest high-volume sites first, so a failure in wave 1 cost two sites of credibility, not six; buy-in came from requirements signed by customer planning, Flextronics staffing the call because it was their protection, and the wave economics accepted up front. I refused auto-loading revisions, forcing one lead-time semantics across sites, and going live on a date instead of a matched report — ATP and lead-time meaning genuinely differ between MTO and MTS; one forced semantics lies about both.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: CM sites with planning-grade BOM 0 → 6 in 3 waves / 5 months; zero auto-loaded overlaps; hardest site in plan. $ impact: ≈$0.3M/yr modeled.

  • $ — ≈$0.3M/yr modeled (manual BOM reconciliation labor, expedites fired at phantom shortages, overlap rework avoided); the record has no dollar for this story — the $1.5M belongs to S8, the program this backbone enabled.
  • # — Contract-manufacturer sites with planning-grade BOM 0 → 6, in 3 waves (record); zero auto-loaded revision overlaps — ever (record).
  • Time — 5 months for six sites; hardest site 6 weeks vs the 3-week average — priced up front and absorbed in plan (record).
  • Customer — Phantom double-timing eliminated at source, so planners stopped chasing false shortages that were never real; the weekly Flextronics call left standing as their forum (record).
  • Quality — The reconciliation report was the per-site go-live gate, not a date on a slide; mapping tables handed to named site super-users; completeness flag written as a rule in the ingest layer (record).

Against the goal stated above: planning-grade sites 0 → 6 in three waves and five months; zero auto-loaded overlaps, ever; phantom double-timing eliminated at source — and the backbone handed to the 200-lane program (S8). The trade was speed, never quality: three waves over five months instead of a big bang, so one surprise could not spend six sites — and the hostile site ran six weeks against a three-week average, priced up front.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Learn and Be Curious?

Because I learned the CM's data by math, not meetings — sampled finished goods, exploded their BOMs in our model, compared component counts to Flextronics' own MRP, and their engineers confirmed the phantom signature the mismatches revealed. Reconcile to the source's math, not its documentation.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Learn and Be Curious?

S16 — learning the stack to build the Snowflake pipeline — is the backup; the MIT toolkit (labeled) and E7 forecasting are the standing third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. For a current-role record of the same curiosity, S4 is the one: my own program's reports indicted my rule and I learned the promise math to fix it.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Sites 0 → 6 with zero auto-loaded overlaps is the record that matters — planning-grade data where there was none, in five months. The downstream dollar deliberately lives elsewhere: this backbone enabled the p99 lane program, and the $1.5M belongs to S8.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

No YoY — the ≈$0.3M/yr is modeled (reconciliation labor, false-shortage expedites, overlap rework), deliberately small because the big dollar belongs to the program this backbone enabled, and I won't double-count it. The record movement is sites 0 → 6 and overlaps at zero.

Probing — I'm concerned about the 360 false shortages — tell me more.

Illustrative volume at the “phantom shows a shortage of 100” pattern, scaled. To firm it I would join the expedite ledger to the shortage alerts for the year before ingest and count how many alerts had no real component gap behind them.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Reconcile to the source's math, not its documentation. Differently: put the completeness flag on the extract from wave 1 — the unflagged partial extract is the failure that looks like thousands of phantom deletions.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Vendor and manufacturer master data at scale carries the same two traps — a phantom level in a kit BOM and an effectivity overlap on an engineering change both manufacture phantom demand. Detect by the source's own math, quarantine with a human disposition, and let the reconciliation report — not a date — open each wave.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The ask was planning-grade BOM and supply visibility at the CM sites — delivered 0 → 6 in three waves and five months, zero auto-loaded overlaps, the hardest site absorbed in plan, and the backbone handed to the 200-lane program (S8).

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you find the phantoms?

By math, not documentation: sampled finished goods, exploded their BOMs in our model, compared component counts to Flextronics' own MRP. The mismatches were the phantom signature; their engineers confirmed.

How did you know discovery was done?

The two traps — phantoms and revision overlaps — named with evidence, the six sites' definitions catalogued, and Flextronics confirming the signature.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at sign-off?

Phantoms ingested as stocked, revision overlaps auto-loaded, and partial extracts consumed. The first two had mechanisms from day one — marker and quarantine; the third got its completeness flag properly at wave 3, my do-differently.

How did you get an external supplier to staff a standing call?

By making the call theirs: it protected Flextronics from blame for misses their feed didn't cause. Evidence was my only leverage — I owned no commercial relationship.

What was the entry bar for a site?

Extracts flowing, a named super-user for every mapping table, and the reconciliation report built for that site. No super-user, no wave slot.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a site?

Reconciliation report matched within the published tolerance — the report was the gate, not a date on a slide. I won't quote the tolerance value; the record doesn't carry it.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The wave-2 yield gap: several points between their report and ours. Planners, Flextronics and engineers in one session traced it to engineering-sample pulls netted on their side versus gross started-units on ours — fixed in one mapping row.

Cutover — Which wave did you hold and how did the business take it?

Wave 3 ran six weeks against a three-week average because the no-CDC ERP needed snapshot-diffing. I had priced it up front, so leadership took it as the plan, not a slip.

What did each wave deliver?

W1: the normalization layer, quarantine, and the first two sites. W2: parameterized mapping tables and three more sites. W3: snapshot-diffing and the hardest site. Each wave's mapping tables stayed with a named super-user.

Hypercare — How did you decide it was closed? What did you leave behind?

Six sites live behind matched reports, zero auto-loaded overlaps, the weekly call running without me. Left behind: a named super-user per mapping table, the quarantine queue with human disposition, the completeness-flag contract, the call as their forum. The model stayed standard; the mappings stayed theirs.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you refuse?

Nowhere on quality; I traded speed — three waves over five months instead of a big bang, so one surprise could not spend six sites. Refused: auto-loading revisions, forcing one lead-time semantics, going live on a date instead of a matched report.

Why not force one lead-time semantics across sites?

ATP and lead-time meaning genuinely differ between MTO and MTS; one forced semantics lies about both.

Do differently?

Put the completeness flag on the extract from wave 1 — the unflagged partial extract is the failure that looks like thousands of phantom deletions.

Where this lands at Amazon: vendor and manufacturer master data at scale carries the same two traps — a phantom level in a kit BOM and an effectivity overlap on an engineering change both manufacture phantom demand. The transferable rules: detect by the source's own math, quarantine with a human disposition, and let the reconciliation report — not a date — open each wave.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"I do not claim a dollar figure from the record on this one; the million and a half of safety stock belongs to the lane program this backbone made possible. What I can model is about three hundred thousand a year: roughly eighty thousand in manual BOM reconciliation the quarantine queue replaced across six sites, about two hundred thousand in expedites that used to be triggered by phantom shortages that were never real, and a small piece, twenty thousand or so, in plans that no longer get reworked because a revision overlap was auto-loaded. The six sites, the five months and the zero auto-loaded overlaps are measured; every dollar here is my model on benchmark rates."

If challenged — the full answers.

The expedite line is the hardest

It assumes about seventy expedites a year were being fired at phantom shortages, one in five of the false signals; to firm it I would join the expedite ledger to the shortage alerts for the year before ingest and count how many alerts had no real component gap behind them.

Where is the record dollar

There is none for this story, on purpose; the inventory release was measured downstream in the p99 program and I will not book it twice, so what I offer here is the labor and expedite model, labelled as such.

Six hours a week per site is a guess

It is; the honest replacement is the disposition-queue log we left behind, which timestamps every human decision, so a month of that log against the pre-ingest planner timesheets would give you the real before and after.

S10 — Elementum: Austria — "the noise said carriers; the data said measurement" (routing-guide rules engine, 25 sites, ~$8M)

Amazon LPs: Are Right, A Lot (primary) · Dive Deep (backup) · Broad Responsibility (primary — one site's fix rolled to 25 sites) · Earn Trust (the Finance-signed method). Crosswalk: not in the loop packet — safe anywhere. Guardrails: say $8M, never "$1M"; "~99% in 3 months," never "two months"; 1–2 hrs/wk is the after, never the before.

Honesty label. I owned the analysis and the rules design and pulled the data myself; the data platform team built the engine; Finance signed the savings method before any dollar was claimed. Site-wave counts and dates beyond "Austria first, then 25 sites" are not in the record.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Austria transportation could not measure on-time delivery; reports said data quality was 40%; the team spent 8–10 hours a week reconciling by hand; everyone believed the carriers were late. Task. I owned the analysis and the rules design. Action. Discovery: three years of shipment history in SQL — true DQ ~55%, not 40; defect classes named. Plan & Design: promised-delivery-date as a routing-guide rules engine with Incoterm in the key (~8% of lanes); Finance-signed savings method before any dollar — contracted guide rate minus paid rate, executed only. Build & Test: alerts by reason code; automated OTD vs a manual sample per cohort before any scorecard. Cutover: Austria scorecard released; manual 8–10 → 1–2 hrs/wk. Hypercare: super-users per region, a one-page runbook; 25 EU sites. Lesson: measure before you blame; sign the method before the number. Result. Primary metric: routing-guide data quality — ~55% true (40% reported) → ~99% in 3 months. $ impact: $500K/yr at Austria (10% of a ~$5M base, Finance-signed executed-only method), then ~$8M across 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes — both record; plus ≈$0.5M/yr of modeled analyst capacity."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — Transportation at the customer's Austria site could not measure on-time delivery. Reports said data quality was 40%; the team spent 8–10 hours a week reconciling promised vs actual dates by hand; the freight base was ≈$5M/yr with no enforceable OTD. Everyone — the site, the carriers' account teams, my own sponsor — believed the carriers were late.

T — I owned the analysis and the rules design.

The goal: an OTD the carriers could not dispute, and savings claimed only on a method Finance signed first. The real challenge: the data had to overturn the room's explanation. Left alone: blaming carriers off bad measurement — a scorecard on 55% data the carriers would dispute and win — the manual reconciliation continuing at every site, and any savings claim disputed after the fact.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: routing-guide data quality — 40% reported, ~55% true once measured; manual reconciliation — 8–10 hours a week per site; enforceable OTD — none, on the ≈$5M/yr freight base.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery — pulled three years of shipment history in SQL myself; ran the defect census (missing rates, wrong-grain lanes, five spellings of one carrier name, brochure transit times); established true DQ ~55% vs 40% reported; reframed the problem as measurement, not carriers. Exit bar: sponsor accepts the reframe on the evidence; defect classes named and counted.
  2. Plan & Design — rules-engine spec (lookup by ship-from/to, mode, volume, Incoterm, SLA; a no-hit pages a human, never a default carrier); reason-code alert design; the savings method signed with Finance before any dollar was claimed (contracted guide rate − paid rate, per shipment, executed only); the scorecard DQ gate (automated OTD = manual sample per lane cohort). Exit bar: spec signed by transportation; Finance method signed; scorecard gate published.
  3. Build & Test — held engineering to the spec; weekly defect burn-down by reason code; manual-sample comparison per lane cohort. Exit bar: DQ trending to ~99%; automated OTD matches the manual sample per cohort.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (Austria) — released the carrier scorecard only after the gate passed; retired the manual reconciliation (8–10 → 1–2 hrs/wk); stood up the savings ledger per shipment. Exit bar: a scorecard the carriers could not dispute; $500K/yr on the Finance-signed method.
  5. Hypercare / Close → scale — super-user model per region; one-page runbook (add a lane · read a no-hit · when to escalate); two shadowed rule reviews per site; rolled out site by site. Exit bar: 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes ≈ $8M; ~99% DQ; adoption cost per site = a rules file + one DQ cycle, in days.

I pulled the three-year shipment history in SQL myself — the room already had an explanation, and SQL was faster than a request queue and could not be argued with; the data platform team built the engine; regional super-users ran the rules files after two shadowed reviews each. Mine beyond the analysis and rules: the SQL pull, the defect census, the rules-engine spec, the reason-code alert design, the Finance-signed savings method and the scorecard gate — the method (contracted guide rate minus paid rate, per shipment, executed only) signed with Finance before the number existed.

The obstacle was a sponsor unhappy with the delay for weeks — answered with the defect classes trending down weekly and the manual-sample gate the scorecard had to pass; he was right to want speed, and the carriers never disputed the result. Incoterm semantics were put into the lane key rather than papered over. Priorities ran evidence before enforcement — the manual reconciliation stayed alive until automated OTD matched the manual sample per lane cohort — and buy-in came from transportation signing the spec, Finance signing the method, and scale going site by site at a rules file plus one DQ cycle each. The decisions challenged: the room's explanation that the carriers were late — overturned with three years of their own data — and I refused a scorecard on 55% data, a silent default carrier on a no-hit, and any savings number without a Finance-signed method.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: DQ ~55% → ~99% in 3 months; $500K/yr Austria → ~$8M across 25 sites (record); manual reconciliation 8–10 → 1–2 hrs/wk.

  • $ — $500K/yr at Austria — 10% of a ~$5M freight base on the Finance-signed, executed-only method — then ~$8M across 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes (record roll-up; ~$320K/site average, derived); plus ≈$0.5M/yr modeled analyst capacity.
  • # — 25 EU sites / 400+ lanes on the same rules file and one DQ cycle each; three years of shipment history pulled in SQL for the baseline (record).
  • % — Routing-guide data quality ~55% true (40% reported) → ~99% in 3 months (record).
  • Time — ~99% DQ in 3 months; adoption cost per site = a rules file + one DQ cycle, in days (record).
  • Customer — A carrier scorecard the carriers could not dispute — automated OTD matched the manual sample per lane cohort before release (record).
  • Quality — Manual reconciliation 8–10 → 1–2 hrs/wk per site; the savings method signed with Finance before any dollar was claimed; negotiated-but-not-executed never counted (record).

Against the goal stated above: DQ ~55 → ~99% in three months; a scorecard the carriers could not dispute; manual reconciliation down from 8–10 to 1–2 hours a week; $500K/yr at Austria on the Finance-signed method and ~$8M across 25 EU sites. The trade was date for evidence: I delayed the scorecard weeks rather than ship it on bad data; scope was never cut.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Are Right, A Lot?

“The noise said carriers; the data said measurement” — I pulled three years of shipment history in SQL myself, established true DQ was ~55%, not 40%, and the fix was a rules engine, not a carrier beating. Being right here meant the data overturning the explanation everyone held.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Are Right, A Lot?

S8 — measured lanes vs brochure — is the backup; S4, the wrong rule corrected right, is the third.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. For the same LP in my current role, S4 is the record: my own rule, indicted by my own program's reports, fixed with promise math.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

DQ ~55 → ~99% in three months is the record that made everything else possible; $500K/yr at Austria and ~$8M across 25 sites are the Finance-signed consequences. The manual hours falling from 8–10 to 1–2 a week is what the team felt.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

Not YoY — the $500K is 10% of a ~$5M freight base on the executed-only method (contracted guide rate minus paid rate, per shipment), and the ~$8M is the same ledger across 25 sites. Negotiated-but-not-executed never counted.

Probing — I'm concerned about the ~$8M roll-up — tell me more.

It is the Finance-signed, executed-only method applied at each site, not a projection; the assumption underneath is that the ten percent Austria rate held elsewhere, and what firms it is the per-shipment ledger at each site — which is what Finance read.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Measure before you blame; sign the method before the number. Differently: start with the industry benchmark for lane transit times instead of proving the brochure wrong lane by lane — same answer, faster.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

This is the model for how a savings claim should be built — method signed with Finance before the number, executed-only, then scaled site by site with the adoption cost measured in days. The Broad Responsibility angle: one site's fix became 25 sites' instrument.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The site needed enforceable OTD; it got a scorecard the carriers could not dispute, DQ at ~99% in three months, $500K/yr on the signed method and ~$8M at network scale — with manual reconciliation down to 1–2 hours a week.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (13). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the three-year pull had every blank classified — missing rate, wrong grain, name variant, brochure time — and the sponsor accepted that true quality was ~55%, not 40%, and that the problem was measurement, not carriers.

Why pull the data yourself?

Because the room already had an explanation. Three years of history in SQL was faster than a request queue and could not be argued with.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Incoterm changing who owns the clock, a silent default carrier on a no-hit, and savings disputed after the fact. Mitigations: Incoterm in the key, page a human, Finance-signed method before any claim.

How did you handle the sponsor objecting to the delay?

I showed the defect classes trending down weekly and the manual-sample gate the scorecard had to pass. He was unhappy for weeks and right to want speed; the carriers never disputed the result.

Who signed the savings method and when?

Finance, before the first dollar was claimed: contracted rate minus paid rate, per shipment, per lane, executed only. Negotiated-but-not-executed never counted.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for a lane rule?

It resolves without no-hits across the sample window and its automated OTD matches the manual sample for its cohort. Until then the manual reconciliation kept running.

How did you treat data defects?

As classes, not one-offs: alerts by reason code, trended weekly, burned down — root cause at scale. That is how 55% became ~99% in three months.

Cutover — What made you say go on the scorecard?

Automated OTD matching the manual sample per lane cohort. Anything less would have shipped a scorecard the carriers could dispute — and they would have won.

What was your rollback?

The manual reconciliation stayed alive until the match held. Reversibility was free because I didn't retire the old process early.

Hypercare — How did you decide Austria was closed and scale could start?

DQ ~99%, manual hours at 1–2 a week, the scorecard undisputed, and a regional super-user running the rules file after two shadowed reviews. Then 25 sites at a rules file plus one DQ cycle each.

What did you hand to the regional super-users?

Their rules file, the one-page runbook — add a lane, read a no-hit, when to escalate — and two shadowed rule reviews each. After that, adding a site cost days.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you refuse?

I traded date for evidence — delayed the scorecard weeks rather than ship it on bad data; scope was never cut. Refused: a scorecard on 55% data, a silent default carrier, and any savings number without a Finance-signed method.

How do you know the $8M is real? · Do differently?

Finance-signed, executed-only method applied across 25 sites; the $500K pilot is 10% of a ~$5M base; anything merely negotiated is excluded. Differently: start with the industry benchmark for lane transit times instead of proving the brochure wrong lane by lane — same answer, faster.

Where this lands at Amazon: the cleanest "used data to overturn the room's explanation" story — and the model for how a savings claim should be built: method signed with Finance before the number, executed-only, then scaled site by site with the adoption cost measured in days. Broad Responsibility angle: one site's fix became 25 sites' instrument.

Total impact statement — say it like this

"The measured numbers are five hundred thousand a year at Austria, ten percent of a five million freight base on a method Finance signed before I claimed a dollar, contracted rate minus paid rate on executed shipments only, and about eight million when the same rules file and one data-quality cycle went to twenty-five sites and four hundred plus lanes. The roll-up is simple: eight million over twenty-five sites is about three hundred twenty thousand a site, which implies roughly eighty million of freight at ten percent, and Austria at five hundred thousand sits above that average because it was a bigger site. On top I model about half a million a year of analyst capacity from cutting manual reconciliation from eight to ten hours a week to one or two at each site; the eight million is record, the half million is my model."

If challenged — the full answers.

Is the eight million really measured

It is the Finance-signed executed-only method applied at each site, not a projection; the assumption underneath is that the ten percent Austria rate held elsewhere, and the thing that firms it is the per-shipment ledger at each site, which is what Finance read.

The half million of labor is the softest

It assumes all twenty-five sites carried Austria's eight to ten hours a week, which I cannot prove; a one-week time log at three sites before their rules file went live would replace that assumption with data.

Why not count negotiated rate cuts too

Because negotiated but not executed is a promise, not a saving; the method counts only shipments that moved at the guide rate, and that discipline is why the carriers never disputed the scorecard or the dollars.

S17 — Elementum: the semi-annual carrier bid — constraint rules and a lane master that cut the cycle from six weeks to about three (no $ claimed)

Amazon LPs: Invent and Simplify (primary) · Frugality (backup) · Dive Deep (the time audit). Crosswalk: loop packet S8 — consistent. Never a dollar on the award — Transportation and Procurement ran it. Never "$500K/yr" or "−25% planning effort."

Honesty label. I owned the requirements, the constraint rules and the parallel run — not the award; transportation, procurement and engineering did not report to me. Procurement joining late is my named miss.
60-second pitch

"Situation. Semi-annual carrier bid: hundreds of lanes, dozens of rate files, six weeks, mostly reconciliation because every carrier named lanes differently. Task. I owned requirements, constraint rules, and the parallel run — not the award. Action. Discovery: sat a full cycle logging hours — the problem class was reconciliation, not optimization. Plan & Design: a lane master; constraints as explicit rules; <5% mismatch vs the last two awards before the optimizer could run; scenario comparison, not a black box. Build & Test: parallel-ran one region's real bid. Cutover: weekly rate-file refresh; a stale banner past 10 days. Hypercare: other regions adopted without a mandate; my miss was procurement joining late. Lesson: diagnose the problem class from a time audit before building anything. Result. Primary metric: carrier-bid cycle time — 6 weeks → ~3 (~50%); pilot region 3 weeks early. $ impact: ≈$190K/yr modeled analyst time returned — the record has no dollar; never savings on the award."

SITUATION / TASK — where it occurred, when it happened, why it is important

S — The customer's transportation team ran a carrier bid twice a year: hundreds of lanes, dozens of carrier rate files, constraints like incumbency floors and volume commitments — six weeks per cycle, mostly reconciliation, because origin/destination naming and mode codes differed per carrier. Everyone assumed it was an optimization problem.

T — Own the requirements, the constraint rules and the parallel run; transportation, procurement and engineering did not report to me.

The goal: a defensible award in half the cycle. The challenge: the diagnosis had to contradict the room — and the proposed cure, a black-box solver, would have produced awards nobody could defend to an incumbent carrier. Left alone: six weeks of analyst time, twice a year, spent reconciling lane names and mode codes instead of analyzing.

The metrics on the table, with their baselines: bid-cycle time — six weeks, twice a year; award defensibility — the bar any tool had to clear, with the optimizer gated behind a <5% back-test against the last two awards; analyst time — the reconciliation hours the lane master existed to return.

ACTION — what I personally owned, how I did it, who else was involved

A — Five gated phases:

  1. Discovery (one full bid cycle) — sat through a whole cycle and built the time audit by activity: the six weeks went to reconciling lane names and mode codes, not analysis; wrote the problem-class statement. Exit bar: Transportation agrees the cost is reconciliation. (Procurement not yet in the room — the miss.)
  2. Plan & Design — the lane-master spec (origin/destination/mode normalization); constraints written one per rule (volume commitments, incumbency floors, mode rules); the validation gate (<5% mismatch vs the last two awards); scenario comparison required as first-class output; the black-box solver rejected in writing. Exit bar: rules signed by Transportation; gate published before any optimizer run.
  3. Build & Test — held engineering to the spec; back-test against the two historical awards; the 10-day stale-data banner required. Exit bar: mismatch <5% against both awards; scenarios explainable to an incumbent.
  4. Cutover & Go-Live (pilot region) — the parallel run — tool vs manual on one region's real bid; acceptance = the tool matches their defensible choices and beats cycle time. Exit bar: pilot region finished 3 weeks early; award defensible in carrier meetings.
  5. Hypercare / Close → adoption — weekly rate-file refresh cadence; Procurement brought into the constraint definitions (late — my miss); the tool handed over as a standing global tool. Exit bar: other regions adopted without a mandate; cycle ~3 weeks; procurement in discovery for the next cycle.

Engineering built the model to the rules; transportation and procurement ran the award, and the regional team's judgment was the acceptance test — the award itself was never mine. Mine specifically: the time audit, the problem-class statement, the lane-master spec, the constraint rules, the validation gate and the parallel-run design. The contribution: sitting through a full bid cycle logging hours — the time audit that contradicted the room — and the lane master plus constraints written one per rule, turning six weeks of reconciliation into configuration; explainability was the spec.

The obstacles: engineering wanted a cheapest-lane solver — answered by making scenario comparison a first-class output and gating the optimizer behind the <5% back-test; constraint semantics differing across functions — “incumbency floor” meant different things to transportation and the category manager — fired because procurement wasn't in the room: my named miss. The back-test read-out classified every mismatch against two historical awards — naming, mode code, constraint semantics — in one session, with owners; buy-in came from rules signed by Transportation, the gate published before any optimizer run, and adoption by envy — other regions came on without a mandate after the pilot finished three weeks early. The decisions challenged: the room's framing that “it's an optimization problem” — overturned by the hour log; the black-box solver, rejected in writing; and the dollar figure on the award, refused — transportation and procurement ran it.

RESULTS — how success was measured: $ · # · % · time · customer · quality

R — Primary metric: cycle 6 weeks → ~3 (~50%); pilot 3 weeks early; adopted without a mandate. ≈$190K/yr modeled analyst hours returned; no dollar on the award.

  • $ — ≈$190K/yr modeled analyst time returned (≈2,200 hrs/yr on illustrative team and region counts; range $35–420K); no dollar on the award — Transportation and Procurement ran it, and never savings on the award.
  • # — Hundreds of lanes, dozens of carrier rate files, twice a year; constraints written one per rule (volume commitments, incumbency floors, mode rules) (record).
  • % — Back-test mismatch <5% vs the last two awards before the optimizer could run — the published gate (record); cycle time down ~50%.
  • Time — Carrier-bid cycle 6 weeks → ~3; pilot region finished 3 weeks early; weekly rate-file refresh with a stale banner past 10 days (record).
  • Customer — Scenario comparison as a first-class output — the award defensible in carrier meetings; other regions adopted without a mandate (record).
  • Quality — The lane master normalized origin/destination/mode naming — the time audit showed reconciliation, not optimization, ate the six weeks; constraint definitions now written with procurement in the room (record).

Against the goal stated above: the carrier-bid cycle six weeks → ~3, twice a year; the pilot region three weeks early; the mismatch gate below 5% against both historical awards; the award defensible in carrier meetings. The trade was mine: a slower first cycle to write and validate constraints, for a tool that halved every cycle after; nothing was traded on the bid itself.

PROBING — QUESTIONS, PHASE DEEP-DIVES, AND THE MATH

Challenge — Why did you choose this story to illustrate Invent and Simplify?

Because the simplification beat the fancier tool: a lane master plus constraints written one per rule, with a <5% back-test gate against the last two awards before any optimizer ran. The invention was the diagnosis — a time audit that showed the problem class was reconciliation, not optimization.

Challenge — What other stories demonstrate Invent and Simplify?

S16 — four systems into one pipeline — is the backup; S3's de-aggregation design is the third, and the §3 E-designs carry the same muscle for design prompts.

Challenge — Could you come up with an example that is more recent?

Elementum years — older. The more recent same-LP records are S16 at Flexport and S3's de-aggregation design in my current role.

Probing — Why did you choose to focus on these results? What other results were important?

Cycle 6 weeks → ~3 is the record, and the pilot finishing three weeks early is what drove adoption without a mandate. The <5% back-test gate is the quality result: an award that survives the incumbent meeting.

Probing — You mentioned dollars — what percentage change is that year over year?

There are no dollars on the award at all — deliberately: transportation and procurement ran it, and the record has no figure. The only dollars I model are ≈$190K/yr of analyst time returned, on illustrative team and region counts, range $35–420K.

Probing — I'm concerned about the three-analysts, three-regions assumption — tell me more.

They are guesses, which is why the range runs from about $35K to about $420K. I'd replace them with the actual bid roster per region and the hour log I kept in discovery — the same audit that found the problem class.

Challenge — What were the lessons learned? What would you have done differently?

Diagnose the problem class from a time audit before building anything. Differently: procurement in discovery on day one — the constraint-semantics risk fired because they weren't.

Challenge — How would you implement this at Amazon?

Invent and Simplify with the diagnosis first — a time audit that contradicted the room, then a simplification (one lane master plus explicit rules) that beat the fancier tool; refusing the dollar I can't defend is what makes the ones I do defend credible.

Challenge — How did these results compare to your actual goals?

The cycle was six weeks; it runs about three, twice a year, with the pilot region three weeks early, the award defensible in carrier meetings, and the mismatch gate below 5% against both historical awards.

PHASE DEEP-PROBE BANK — Q&A (12). Answer the phase asked, then stop.

Discovery — How did you know discovery was done?

When the time audit from a full cycle showed the six weeks were reconciliation — lane names and mode codes differing per carrier — not analysis, and transportation agreed. It was not fully done: procurement was missing, which I learned later.

Why sit through a whole cycle instead of interviewing?

Interviews tell you what people think they do. Logging hours told me where the time actually went, and the answer contradicted the room.

Plan & Design — What was in your risk register at design sign-off?

Constraint semantics differing across functions, a black-box award, and stale rates. The first one fired — "incumbency floor" meant different things to transportation and the category manager — because I hadn't put procurement in the room.

How did you handle engineering objecting to the design?

They wanted a cheapest-lane solver. I made scenario comparison a first-class output and required <5% mismatch against past awards before the optimizer could run. Explainability was the spec.

Build & Test — What was your definition of done for the model?

<5% mismatch against the last two award cycles, every scenario explainable in one sentence, and a stale-data banner past 10 days. Below that bar the optimizer did not run.

Describe your bug-bash equivalent.

The back-test read-out: every mismatch against two historical awards classified — naming, mode code, constraint semantics — in one session, with owners. That is where most of the reconciliation logic got fixed.

Cutover — What was your go criterion for the pilot?

The tool matched the regional team's defensible choices on their real bid and beat the cycle time. Their judgment was the acceptance test, not mine.

What was the rollback?

The manual process ran in parallel through the pilot. If the tool had disagreed indefensibly, they would have awarded by hand — never needed.

Hypercare — How did other regions come on?

Without a mandate — the pilot finished three weeks early and the tool carried each region's own constraints as rules. Adoption by envy; the refresh cadence kept it honest.

What kept the tool honest after the pilot?

The weekly rate-file refresh with the stale banner past 10 days, and constraint definitions now written with procurement in the room. A model with stale rates or unowned rules decays into a spreadsheet with a logo.

Cross-phase — Where did you trade scope for date? What did you refuse?

Nowhere on the bid itself. The trade was mine: a slower first cycle to write and validate constraints, for a tool that halved every cycle after. Refused: a black-box solver — and a dollar figure; transportation and procurement ran the award.

What did it save? · Do differently?

I don't have a dollar figure and won't invent one; the cycle halved and the pilot finished three weeks early — the savings claim belonged to the negotiation. Differently: procurement in discovery on day one.

Where this lands at Amazon: Invent and Simplify with the diagnosis first — a time audit that contradicted the room ("it's an optimization problem") and a simplification (one lane master + explicit rules) that beat the fancier tool. The refusal to claim the award dollar is the credibility move interviewers remember.
